 \alpha=\frac{\mathbb E[D(D-1)]}{d^2}.$$ Thus $\alpha=1-1/d$ for the regular model and $\alpha=1$ for the Poisson model. A subscript $e$ means evaluation at the edge message $m_e=1+\lambda v$. We argue by contradiction, assuming $\mu>0$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### An entropy identity with an additive correction

For a positive message of coordinate average one, define <a id="pos:functionals"></a>$$\label{pos:functionals}
 \begin{aligned}
 I&=\langle m\log m\rangle,& J&=\frac12\langle v\log m\rangle,\\
 u_i&=\log m_i-\langle \log m\rangle,& B&=\frac12\langle m u^2\rangle.
 \end{aligned}$$ Here and below logarithms are natural. The expected additivity of $J$ below is the symmetric-channel form of the symmetrized-entropy recursion of Formentin and Külske (Formentin and Külske 2009, Proposition 3.3, equation (15)) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[FormentinKulske2009Entropy](#ref-FormentinKulske2009Entropy)]: their functional equals $2J$ in our normalization. We prove the identities needed here, including the centered-log correction $B$. Lemma <a href="#fnd:log-integrability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fnd:log-integrability">7</a> gives strictly positive fixed-point coordinates and integrability of these functions: their coordinate factors are bounded, and their logarithmic factors have degree at most two.

Under a fixed input spin $i$, tilting and permutation symmetry give $$\mathbb E[u_{e,i}\mid S=i]=\mathbb E\langle m_eu_e\rangle=2\mathbb EJ_e,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E[u_{e,i}^2\mid S=i]=\mathbb E\langle m_eu_e^2\rangle=2\mathbb EB_e.$$ Centered log coordinates add under products: the common normalizing constant disappears on centering. Conditional independence of the branches therefore gives <a id="pos:additive"></a>$$\label{pos:additive}
 \mathbb EJ=d\mathbb EJ_e,\qquad
 \mathbb EB=d\mathbb EB_e+2\alpha(\mathbb EJ)^2.$$ For clarity, the off-diagonal term in the second formula, before division by two, is $4\mathbb E[D(D-1)](\mathbb EJ_e)^2$.

<a id="pos:functional-F"></a>
<a id="pos:entropy"></a>
For a product of $n$ edge experiments write $Z_n$ for its likelihood normalizer relative to their independent marginal laws, and set $$H_n=\mathbb E_{\mathrm{prod}}[Z_n\log Z_n],\qquad H=\mathbb EH_{D}.$$ This is a relative entropy and is nonnegative. Expanding the logarithm of the normalized output likelihood gives $$\mathbb EI=d\mathbb EI_e-H.$$ Each branch retains its original marginal distribution after the joint likelihood tilt, which justifies the individual terms in this expansion. The combination $$\label{pos:functional-F}
 F=3I-2J+\frac45B$$ therefore satisfies the exact identity $$\label{pos:entropy}
 \mathbb E(F-dF_e)=-3H+\frac85\alpha(\mathbb EJ)^2.$$

<a id="pos:comparison"></a>
The useful feature of this combination is its positive radial defect: for every positive message of coordinate average one, <a id="pos:radial"></a>$$\label{pos:radial}
 F(m)-\lambda^{-2}F(1+\lambda v)
 \ge\frac{1-\lambda^2}{2}x^2\qquad(0<\lambda<1).$$ Lemma <a href="#rad:lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rad:lemma">23</a> proves this pointwise inequality by radial differentiation and explicit polynomial sign decompositions. Since $d=\lambda^{-2}$, its expectation and Equation <a href="#pos:entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:entropy">[pos:entropy]</a> imply $$\label{pos:comparison}
 \frac{1-\lambda^2}{2}\nu+3H
 \le\frac85\alpha(\mathbb EJ)^2.$$ We will contradict this inequality by bounding $\mathbb EJ$ above and $H$ below. The additive identity for $J$ also restricts the possible values of $\mu,\nu,\eta$; we establish those restrictions first.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Moment information from the additive identity

<a id="pos:coordinate-identities"></a>
The cubic identity in Equation <a href="#fnd:cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fnd:cubic">[fnd:cubic]</a>, together with $\langle v\rangle=0$ and $\langle v^2\rangle=2x$, gives the additional moment relation $$\label{pos:coordinate-identities}
 \langle v^4\rangle=3x\langle v^2\rangle+2y\langle v\rangle=6x^2.$$ We also use $|y|\le x^{3/2}$ from Equation <a href="#fnd:invariant-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fnd:invariant-bounds">[fnd:invariant-bounds]</a>.

<a id="stmt-pos-moment-bounds"></a>
<div id="pos:moment-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *Put $$c_0=\frac{37}{27},\qquad q=\frac{c_0}{2+\lambda},\qquad
 c=c_0+2\lambda q.$$ Then <a id="pos:moment-estimates"></a>$$\label{pos:moment-estimates}
 0\le\mathbb EJ\le\mu-\lambda q\nu,
 \qquad c\nu\le\eta\le\sqrt{\mu\nu}.$$ If $s=1$ in the regular model and $s$ is the survival probability in the Poisson model, then <a id="pos:moment-region"></a>$$\label{pos:moment-region}
 \frac{\mu^2}{s}\le\nu\le\frac{\mu}{c^2},\qquad
 \mu\le\frac{s}{c^2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pos-moment-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-pos-moment-bounds</summary>

<a id="pos:vanishing"></a>
<a id="pos:Q-identities"></a>
*Proof.* The integral expression $$J=\frac12\langle v^2\int_0^1\frac{dt}{1+tv}\rangle$$ and $\mathbb EJ=\lambda^{-2}\mathbb EJ_e$ imply $$\label{pos:vanishing}
 \mathbb E\int_0^1t\langle \frac{v^3}{(1+tv)(1+t\lambda v)}\rangle\,dt=0.$$ Indeed, the difference of the two integral expressions for $J$ and $\lambda^{-2}J_e$ is $-(1-\lambda)/2$ times this integrand. Define $$A=\mathbb E\int_0^1t^2\langle \frac{v^4}{1+tv}\rangle\,dt,
 \qquad
 Q=\frac12\mathbb E\int_0^1t^2
       \langle \frac{v^4}{(1+tv)(1+t\lambda v)}\rangle\,dt.$$ Subtracting <a href="#pos:vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:vanishing">[pos:vanishing]</a> from the corresponding integral expressions gives the exact formulas $$\label{pos:Q-identities}
 \mathbb EJ=\mu-\lambda Q,\qquad \eta=A+2\lambda Q.$$ For the first, use $\mu-\lambda^{-2}\mathbb EJ_e
 =\frac\lambda2\mathbb E\int_0^1 t\langle v^3/(1+t\lambda v)\rangle\,dt$. For the second, use $\eta=\mathbb E\int_0^1t\langle v^3\rangle\,dt$. Both subtractions reduce to multiplication by the two positive denominators. All these integrals are absolutely integrable: the powers of $v$ are bounded, $1+t\lambda v\ge1-\lambda$, and integration of the only possibly small denominator $1+tv$ is controlled by $1+|\log m_i|$. Thus the previous logarithmic bounds justify the interchanges of expectations and integrals.

For $x>0$, put $r=\sqrt{x}<1$ and $k=y/r^3\in[-1,1]$. Using Equations <a href="#fnd:cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fnd:cubic">[fnd:cubic]</a> and <a href="#pos:coordinate-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:coordinate-identities">[pos:coordinate-identities]</a>, <a id="pos:h-bound"></a>$$\label{pos:h-bound}
 \begin{split}
 h(t)&=x^{-2}\langle \frac{v^4}{1+tv}\rangle
   =\frac{6-10tr k+4t^2r^2k^2}{1-3t^2r^2+2t^3r^3k}\\
 &\ge\frac{6-4tr}{1+tr-2t^2r^2}
  \ge4+\frac{64}{9}(tr-\tfrac12)^2.
 \end{split}$$ To verify the first inequality, write $z=tr<1$. The numerator is positive and decreases as $k$ increases to $1$, while the denominator increases and remains positive. Evaluation at $k=1$ gives the displayed fraction. For the second inequality use $$\frac{6-4z}{1+z-2z^2}
   =4+\frac{8(z-1/2)^2}{1+z-2z^2},\qquad
 0<1+z-2z^2\le\frac98.$$ Since $$\int_0^1t^2(tr-\tfrac12)^2\,dt
   =\frac{(r-5/8)^2}{5}+\frac1{192},$$ integration gives $\int_0^1t^2h(t)\,dt\ge37/27=c_0$. Consequently $A\ge c_0\nu$.

At each $t$, assign the three coordinates weights proportional to $v_i^4/(1+tv_i)$. Their weighted mean of $tv_i$ is $$\frac{\langle v^4\rangle-\langle v^4/(1+tv)\rangle}{\langle v^4/(1+tv)\rangle}
 =\frac6{h(t)}-1\le\frac12.$$ The function $z\mapsto(1+\lambda z)^{-1}$ is convex and decreasing on the relevant interval. Jensen’s inequality therefore yields $$\langle \frac{v^4}{(1+tv)(1+t\lambda v)}\rangle
 \ge\frac{2}{2+\lambda}\langle \frac{v^4}{1+tv}\rangle.$$ Messages with $x=0$ contribute zero throughout. Integrating proves $Q\ge A/(2+\lambda)\ge q\nu$. Now $J\ge0$ pointwise, because $v_i\log(1+v_i)\ge0$. Equations <a href="#pos:Q-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:Q-identities">[pos:Q-identities]</a>, followed by Cauchy–Schwarz, prove $$0\le\mathbb EJ\le\mu-\lambda q\nu,
 \qquad c\nu\le\eta\le\mathbb Ex^{3/2}\le\sqrt{\mu\nu}.$$ Finally, Lemma <a href="#fnd:survival" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fnd:survival">8</a> gives $\mu^2\le s\nu$. Squaring $c\nu\le\sqrt{\mu\nu}$ gives $\nu\le\mu/c^2$, and combining the two bounds gives the remaining assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Correlation generated by successive branches

<a id="stmt-pos-correlation-bound"></a>
<div id="pos:correlation-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="pos:H-bound"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *In either offspring model, $$\label{pos:H-bound}
 \frac{3H}{\alpha}\ge
 \frac{3\mu^2}{2(1+2\mu/3)}-\frac\lambda{\sqrt2}\mu\nu.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pos-correlation-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-pos-correlation-bound</summary>

<a id="pos:increment"></a>
*Proof.* First consider two independent marginal experiments with posterior means $\zeta_0$ and $\zeta_e$, the latter an edge experiment. Their product normalizer is $1+z$, where $$z=\zeta_0\overline{\zeta_e}+\overline{\zeta_0}\zeta_e.$$ Writing $\mu_0=\mathbb E|\zeta_0|^2$ and $\eta_0=\mathbb E\Re(\zeta_0^3)$, rotation and reflection symmetry give $$\mathbb Ez=0,\qquad \mathbb Ez^2=2\mu_0\lambda^2\mu,
 \qquad \mathbb Ez^3=2\eta_0\lambda^3\eta.$$ For $z>-1$, Taylor’s theorem, with fourth derivative $2/(1+z)^3>0$, gives $$(1+z)\log(1+z)\ge z+\frac{z^2}{2}-\frac{z^3}{6}.$$ The inequality extends continuously to $z=-1$. Thus the correlation increment $C_0$, itself a nonnegative relative entropy, satisfies $$\label{pos:increment}
 3C_0\ge3\lambda^2\mu\mu_0-\lambda^3\eta\eta_0.$$ The increments telescope to total correlation by the identity for $I$.

For the regular model, the partial experiment with a fixed number $j\le d$ of branches is degraded from the full fixed-point experiment by discarding branches. For the Poisson model, the partial experiment is the whole mixture with observed count $N_t\sim\operatorname{Pois}(t)$, $0\le t\le d$; thinning the full experiment produces this mixture. The degradation comparison in the Poisson case is applied to this observed-count mixture. Conditional Jensen for the convex function $|\zeta|^4$ consequently gives $\mathbb E|\zeta_0|^4\le\nu$, and hence $$\eta_0\le\sqrt{\mu_0\nu}.$$ There is also a useful lower bound on $\mu_0$. Put $\beta=\lambda^2\mu$. Each edge posterior mean $\zeta_e$ has $$\mathbb E[\zeta_e\mid S=i]=i\beta,\qquad
 \mathbb E[\zeta_e\overline S]=\mathbb E|\zeta_e|^2=\beta.$$ The first identity follows by tilting by the edge likelihood and using rotation symmetry. For the sum $T_j$ of $j$ edge posterior means, conditional independence therefore gives $$\mathbb E[T_j\overline S]=j\beta,\qquad
 \mathbb E|T_j|^2=j\beta+j(j-1)\beta^2.$$ Conditioning on the entire partial observation and applying Cauchy–Schwarz gives <a id="pos:projection"></a>$$\label{pos:projection}
 \mu_j\ge L_j:=\frac{j\beta}{1+(j-1)\beta}\qquad(j\ge1).$$ Here $\mu_j$ denotes its posterior second moment.

<a id="pos:truncated-bound"></a>
We explain carefully how to substitute this lower bound into <a href="#pos:increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:increment">[pos:increment]</a>. Since $0\le\eta\le\sqrt{\mu\nu}$, any partial product with $\mu_0\ge L>0$ satisfies $$3C_0\ge3\lambda^2\mu\mu_0
  \left(1-\frac{\lambda\nu}{3\sqrt{\mu\mu_0}}\right).$$ The factor in parentheses is increasing in $\mu_0$. If it is nonnegative at $L$, both factors can be bounded below at $L$. If it is negative there, the same resulting lower bound follows from $C_0\ge0$. In either case, $$\label{pos:truncated-bound}
 3C_0\ge3\lambda^2\mu L-\lambda^3\sqrt\mu\,\nu\sqrt L.$$

In the regular case use $L=L_j$, and note $\sqrt{L_j}\le\lambda\sqrt{j\mu}$. Summing the increments yields $$3H\ge3\lambda^4\mu^2\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}
       \frac{j}{1+(j-1)\lambda^2\mu}
       -\lambda^4\mu\nu\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\sqrt j.$$ Weighted Jensen, with weights proportional to $j$, uses $$\lambda^2\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}j(j-1)}{\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}j}
 =\frac{2(d-2)}{3d}\le\frac23,
 \qquad \lambda^4\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}j=\frac\alpha2.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\frac{\lambda^4}{\alpha}\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\sqrt j
 \le\frac{\lambda^4}{\alpha}
       \sqrt{(d-1)\frac{d(d-1)}2}
 =\frac\lambda{\sqrt2}.$$ These are precisely <a href="#pos:H-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:H-bound">[pos:H-bound]</a>.

For the Poisson model, let $N_t\sim\mathrm{Pois}(t)$, $0\le t\le d$, and let $h(t)=\mathbb EH_{N_t}$. Because $H_n=n\mathbb EI_e-\mathbb EI_n$ and $0\le I_n,I_e\le\log3$ in expectation, $0\le H_n\le n\log3$. The Poisson series can therefore be differentiated termwise on bounded intervals, giving $$h'(t)=\mathbb E(H_{N_t+1}-H_{N_t}).$$ Thus $h'(t)$ is the expected correlation increment on adding one edge to the partial experiment of intensity $t$. Indeed, if $Z_n$ is the normalizer of the first $n$ branches and $Z_{n,e}$ is the normalizer for their posterior experiment paired with the new edge, then $Z_{n+1}=Z_n Z_{n,e}$. Integrating the logarithm of this identity under the full likelihood tilt gives $H_{n+1}-H_n=\mathbb E[Z_{n,e}\log Z_{n,e}]$, where the expectation on the right is under the independent partial and edge marginals. Averaging over the observed Poisson count gives the asserted increment. The same projection proof, now using $\mathbb EN_t=t$ and $\mathbb EN_t(N_t-1)=t^2$, gives $$\mu_t\ge L_t:=\frac{t\beta}{1+t\beta}.$$ Thinning proves the required degradation and fourth-moment bound. Applying <a href="#pos:truncated-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:truncated-bound">[pos:truncated-bound]</a> for $t>0$, integrating from $0$ to $d$, and using $h(0)=0$, gives $$3H\ge3\lambda^4\mu^2\int_0^d\frac{t\,dt}{1+t\lambda^2\mu}
        -\lambda^4\mu\nu\int_0^d\sqrt t\,dt.$$ Weighted Jensen with weights $t\,dt$ uses $\lambda^2\int_0^dt^2\,dt/\int_0^dt\,dt=2/3$, while $\lambda^4\int_0^dt\,dt=1/2$. The loss equals $(2/3)\lambda\mu\nu$, which is at most $\lambda\mu\nu/\sqrt2$. As $\alpha=1$, this proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The strict contradiction

<a id="stmt-pos-nonreconstruction"></a>
<div id="pos:nonreconstruction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13**. *At every positive equality parameter $d\lambda^2=1$, the limiting posterior is uniform in both tree models. Consequently reconstruction is impossible for $0\le\lambda\le d^{-1/2}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pos-nonreconstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-pos-nonreconstruction</summary>

<a id="pos:before-scalar"></a>
<a id="pos:scalar-necessary"></a>
<a id="pos:c-lower"></a>
*Proof.* Suppose $\mu>0$. Since $0\le\mathbb EJ\le\mu-\lambda q\nu$ and $\nu\le\mu/c^2$, $$(\mathbb EJ)^2\le\mu^2-\lambda q\mu\nu
                  \left(2-\frac{\lambda q}{c^2}\right).$$ Substituting this estimate and Equation <a href="#pos:H-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:H-bound">[pos:H-bound]</a> into Equation <a href="#pos:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:comparison">[pos:comparison]</a> gives $$\label{pos:before-scalar}
 \left(\frac{1-\lambda^2}{2\alpha}+g\lambda\mu\right)\nu
  +\frac{3\mu^2}{2(1+2\mu/3)}\le\frac85\mu^2,
 \qquad
 g=\frac85q\left(2-\frac{\lambda q}{c^2}\right)-\frac1{\sqrt2}.$$ Here $$q\ge\frac{c_0}{3},\qquad
 \frac{\lambda q}{(c_0+2\lambda q)^2}\le\frac1{8c_0},$$ and the second inequality is equivalent to $(c_0-2\lambda q)^2\ge0$. Consequently $$g\ge\frac{113}{81}-\frac1{\sqrt2}>\frac{17}{25}.$$ For the strict comparison, both sides of $113/81-17/25>1/\sqrt2$ are positive, and their squares compare as $1448^2/2025^2>1/2$. Since $\alpha\le1$ and $\nu\ge\mu^2/s$, <a href="#pos:before-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:before-scalar">[pos:before-scalar]</a> implies $$\label{pos:scalar-necessary}
 T:=\frac{1-\lambda^2}{2s}
       +\frac{3}{2(1+2\mu/3)}
       +\frac{17\lambda\mu}{25s}\le\frac85.$$ We show that this is impossible using $0<s\le1$, $\mu\le s/c^2$, and $$\label{pos:c-lower}
 c\ge\underline c(\lambda):=c_0(1+2\lambda/3).$$

<a id="pos:small-sufficient"></a>
First suppose $0<\lambda\le3/10$. Dropping the last term of $T$ and using $\mu\le1/c^2$, it suffices that $$\label{pos:small-sufficient}
 \left(\frac25-\frac{\lambda^2}{2}\right)c^2
 >\frac23\left(\frac{11}{10}+\frac{\lambda^2}{2}\right).$$ Indeed, this is exactly the inequality obtained by requiring $(1-\lambda^2)/2+(3/2)/(1+2/(3c^2))>8/5$. By <a href="#pos:c-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:c-lower">[pos:c-lower]</a>, the left side of <a href="#pos:small-sufficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:small-sufficient">[pos:small-sufficient]</a> is at least $$c_0^2\left[\frac25+
  \left(\frac8{15}-\frac{3}{20}\left(\frac65\right)^2\right)
       \lambda\right]
 =\frac{2738}{3645}+\frac{162911}{273375}\lambda.$$ Here we used $\lambda^2\le(3/10)\lambda$ and $1+2\lambda/3\le6/5$, and discarded a nonnegative quadratic term. The right side is at most $11/15+\lambda/10$. The constant terms have difference $13/729>0$, and $162911/273375>1/10$, proving <a href="#pos:small-sufficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:small-sufficient">[pos:small-sufficient]</a>.

Next suppose $3/10\le\lambda\le1/\sqrt2$. Since $$\frac{3}{2(1+2\mu/3)}\ge\frac32-\mu,$$ we have $$T-\frac85\ge\frac25-\frac{\lambda^2}{2}
             -\left(1-\frac{17\lambda}{25}\right)\mu
 \ge\frac25-\frac{\lambda^2}{2}
             -\frac{1-17\lambda/25}{c^2}.$$ Thus it suffices to prove $$f(\lambda):=\left(\frac25-\frac{\lambda^2}{2}\right)
                \underline c(\lambda)^2
 >1-\frac{17\lambda}{25}.$$ The difference of these two functions is concave, because $$f''(\lambda)=-\frac{1369}{32805}
                   (120\lambda^2+180\lambda+29)<0.$$ At the first endpoint, $f(3/10)=194398/202500>19/20$, whereas $1-(17/25)(3/10)=199/250<4/5$. At the other endpoint, $$f(1/\sqrt2)=\frac{1369}{729}
                 \left(\frac{11}{60}+\frac{\sqrt2}{10}\right)
 >\frac35,
 \qquad
 1-\frac{17}{25\sqrt2}<\frac{13}{25}.$$ The first comparison follows already from $\sqrt2>7/5$; the second from $1/\sqrt2>707/1000$. Concavity now proves the strict inequality on the entire interval.

Finally suppose $1/\sqrt2<\lambda<1$, which can occur at equality only in the Poisson model. Lemma <a href="#fnd:survival" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fnd:survival">8</a>, with $d=\lambda^{-2}$, gives $s\le2(1-\lambda^2)$. Hence $$T-\frac85\ge\frac3{20}
                  -\frac{1-17\lambda/25}{c^2}.$$ It remains to check $(3/20)\underline c(\lambda)^2>1-17\lambda/25$. At $\lambda=1/\sqrt2$ this is precisely the strict endpoint inequality just proved. Its left side increases with $\lambda$, while its right side decreases, so it holds throughout the remaining interval.

All cases contradict <a href="#pos:scalar-necessary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:scalar-necessary">[pos:scalar-necessary]</a>. Thus $\mu=0$, which makes the limiting posterior uniform. Same-sign degradation extends the conclusion to smaller positive parameters, and $\lambda=0$ is the independent channel. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:local"></a>
### Negative equality: exclusion of small fixed points

Throughout this section $\lambda=-a$, $0<a\le 1/2$, and $d=a^{-2}\ge4$. The offspring count is either the constant integer $d$ or $\operatorname{Pois}(d)$. We consider a permutation-invariant fixed point of the posterior recursion and use $$x=|\zeta|^2,\qquad y=\Re(\zeta^3),\qquad
 \mu=\mathbb Ex,\quad \nu=\mathbb Ex^2,\quad \eta=\mathbb Ey.$$ All finite decimals in this section and the next denote exact rational numbers. In particular, none of the strict comparisons below is a floating-point comparison.

<a id="stmt-loc-gap"></a>
<div id="loc:gap" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14** (Local gap at negative equality). *<span id="loc:criterion" label="loc:criterion"></span>Every nontrivial permutation-invariant fixed point at $da^2=1$ satisfies $\mu>1/20$. This includes $a=1/2$ in both offspring models.*

</div>

We first establish the pair inequalities used in the proof. If a current message has invariants $(X,y_0)$ and an edge message has invariants $(z,B_0)$, define $d_x,d_e,d_n$ to be the output means of $x,y,x^2$ minus $X+z,y_0+B_0,X^2+z^2$, respectively. The output mean here averages the six relative label permutations with their likelihood tilt. Conditional on the permutation orbits of two independent symmetric marginal messages, these are exactly the relative orientations of their product experiment. If the child before the edge has invariants $(w,Y)$, then $$z=a^2w,\qquad B_0=-a^3Y.$$ Set $$S_A=X^2z,\quad S_B=Xz^2,\quad S=S_A+S_B,\quad
 W=y_0z+XB_0,\quad C=\frac52.$$ (The letter $W$ in this section denotes only this pair expression.)

<a id="stmt-loc-pair"></a>
<div id="loc:pair" class="lemma">

<a id="loc:coarsepair"></a>
<a id="loc:xpair"></a>
<a id="loc:kpair"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Certified pair inequalities). *For any current and child posterior vectors, including vectors on the boundary of the simplex, and any $0\le a\le1/2$, <a id="loc:ypair"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 d_n&\le \frac{13}{2}Xz,\qquad
 -\frac{79}{250}Xz\le d_e\le11Xz,\label{loc:coarsepair}\\
 d_x&\le-Xz-2W+2y_0B_0+\frac{51}{20}S,\label{loc:xpair}\\
 6Xz-6W+y_0B_0-\frac{19}{2}S
 &\le d_e\le6Xz-6W+y_0B_0+S,\label{loc:ypair}\\
 C d_n-2d_e&\le-2Xz+22W-7y_0B_0+26S_A+\frac{111}{2}S_B.
 \label{loc:kpair}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-loc-pair"><summary>Proof of stmt-loc-pair</summary>

*Proof.* For $a=1/2$ and strictly positive input vectors, Section <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:pair">11.1</a> proves the inequalities by coefficientwise nonnegativity of fourteen integer polynomials. That derivation identifies each cleared polynomial with a pair inequality, including the likelihood tilt and the cancellation between the two reflection classes. We now extend those inequalities to all the inputs in the statement.

For general $a\in[0,1/2]$, replace the child vector $m=1+v$ by $1+2av$, a valid convex mixture with the uniform likelihood vector. Its coloring edge is $1-av$, so the same certificate gives the desired edge invariants $z=a^2w$ and $B_0=-a^3Y$.

Finally approximate boundary vectors by positive mixtures with the uniform vector. For each relative permutation, write $Z$ and $m^{\mathrm{out}}$ for the normalizer and output likelihood vector in Equation <a href="#fnd:product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fnd:product">[fnd:product]</a>. Its contribution to a tilted moment is $Zf(m^{\mathrm{out}})/6$, where $f=x,y,x^2$ is bounded and continuous on the simplex. This contribution is continuous when $Z>0$ and tends to zero as $Z\to0$. Thus the tilted expectations extend continuously over every zero-normalizer orientation, as do the polynomial right-hand sides. Taking limits proves the full closed simplex statement, including $a=1/2$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-loc-pair-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-loc-pair</summary>

<a id="loc:averages"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#loc:gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:gap">14</a>.* Suppose $0<\mu\le m_0:=1/20$. We introduce a common notation for summing partial-product increments. In the regular model let $(\mu_j,\nu_j,\eta_j)$ be the moments from $j$ edge branches, $s=j/d$, $0\le j\le d$, and set $$\operatorname{av} f_j=\frac1d\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}f_j,
 \qquad \alpha=1-\frac1d.$$ A normalized increment is $d$ times the increment from $j$ to $j+1$. In the Poisson model the partial product has $\operatorname{Pois}(sd)$ branches, $0\le s\le1$; the subscript $j$ below denotes this partial product, $\operatorname{av}$ means integration in $s$ over $[0,1]$, and $\alpha=1$. Its normalized increment is the derivative in $s$. Differentiating the Poisson series for the bounded functions $x,y,x^2$ gives $d$ times the expected single-branch increment; absolute convergence justifies this differentiation. In both models the endpoint is the fixed-point law, the initial moments vanish, and $$\label{loc:averages}
 \operatorname{av}s=\frac\alpha2,\qquad
 \operatorname{av}s^2\le\frac\alpha3.$$ For regular counts the second average is $(d-1)(2d-1)/(6d^2)$; for Poisson it is $1/3$.

At equality the standalone edge contributions to $x$ sum to $da^2\mu=\mu$, which is already the fixed-point endpoint moment. The average of the actual normalized pair excess in $x$ is therefore zero. We will contradict this identity by showing that the pair upper bound has negative average when $\mu\le m_0$. To control its higher moments, we first bound the partial-product moments and then determine the sign of $\eta$.

<a id="loc:partialmu"></a>
<a id="loc:ratio"></a>
The projection bound gives, respectively, $$\mu_j\ge\frac{j a^2\mu}{1+(j-1)a^2\mu},\qquad
 \mu_j\ge\frac{s\mu}{1+s\mu}.$$ For completeness, one edge posterior complex mean has correlation $a^2\mu$ with the spin, conditional mean $a^2\mu$ times that spin, and second moment $a^2\mu$. The sum of $j$ independent branches conditional on the spin consequently has second moment $ja^2\mu+j(j-1)(a^2\mu)^2$ and correlation $ja^2\mu$. Conditional expectation followed by Cauchy–Schwarz proves the first bound. Poisson factorial moments $sd$ and $(sd)^2$ prove the second. It follows that $$\label{loc:partialmu}
 (s-m_0s^2)\mu\le\mu_j\le1.14s\mu.$$ Here is a derivation of the upper bound. Every simplex message satisfies $$\label{loc:ratio}
 -\frac{x}{2}\le y\le x.$$ For $x>0$ this is Equation <a href="#fnd:ratio-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fnd:ratio-domain">[fnd:ratio-domain]</a>, and at $x=0$ both sides vanish by Equation <a href="#fnd:invariant-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fnd:invariant-bounds">[fnd:invariant-bounds]</a>. Thus, when $Xz>0$, $$-\tfrac12\le y_0/X\le1,\qquad
 -\tfrac12\le B_0/z=-aY/w\le\tfrac14,\qquad z\le\tfrac14.$$ Checking the four corners of this ratio rectangle gives $-2(r+t)+2rt\le5/2$. Equation <a href="#loc:xpair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:xpair">[loc:xpair]</a> therefore gives $$\frac{d_x}{Xz}\le-1+\frac52+\frac{51}{20}(1+1/4)
       =\frac{75}{16}<5.$$ If $Xz=0$, one input is uniform and the excess vanishes. Hence the pair gain of $x$ is at most $z(1+5X)$. Iterating, or solving the corresponding differential inequality, gives $\mu_j\le(e^{5s\mu}-1)/5$. The function $(e^t-1)/t$ increases for $t\ge0$, and $$e^{1/4}\le1+\frac14+\frac1{32}
       +\frac{(1/4)^3/6}{1-1/16}
       =\frac{1849}{1440}<1.285.$$ This proves the upper bound in <a href="#loc:partialmu" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:partialmu">[loc:partialmu]</a>.

<a id="loc:partialnu"></a>
<a id="loc:partialeta"></a>
The standalone normalized increments in $x,y,x^2$ are $\mu,-a\eta,a^2\nu$, respectively. The partial product and the next edge branch are independent under the product marginal law, and each has a symmetric law. Conditional on their two permutation orbits, Lemma <a href="#loc:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:pair">15</a> therefore applies; averaging its bounds factors every mixed invariant moment. In particular, Equation <a href="#loc:coarsepair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:coarsepair">[loc:coarsepair]</a> gives $$(1-a^2)\nu\le6.5\mu\operatorname{av}\mu_j,
 \qquad
 -.316\mu\operatorname{av}\mu_j
 \le(1+a)\eta\le11\mu\operatorname{av}\mu_j.$$ Using Equations <a href="#loc:partialmu" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:partialmu">[loc:partialmu]</a> and <a href="#loc:averages" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:averages">[loc:averages]</a>, $1-a^2\ge3/4$, $a/(1+a)\le1/3$, and $\alpha\le1$, we obtain <a id="loc:coarse"></a>$$\label{loc:coarse}
 \frac{\nu}{\mu^2}\le4.94,\quad
 \frac{a^2\nu}{\mu^2}\le1.235,\quad
 -.061\le\frac{a\eta}{\mu^2}\le2.09.$$ The endpoint bound for $\nu/\mu^2$ before rounding is $6.5(1.14)\alpha/[2(1-a^2)]$. The upper bound for $\eta/\mu^2$ is $11(1.14)\alpha/[2(1+a)]$, and the bound on its negative magnitude is $.316(1.14)\alpha/[2(1+a)]$. Summing to a partial endpoint also yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\nu_j}{\mu^2}&\le1.235s+3.705s^2,\label{loc:partialnu}\\
 -2.09s-.181s^2
 &\le\frac{\eta_j}{\mu^2}\le .061s+6.27s^2.
 \label{loc:partialeta}
\end{aligned}$$ For example the quadratic coefficients come from $6.5(1.14)/2=3.705$, $.316(1.14)/2=.18012<.181$, and $11(1.14)/2=6.27$. In the discrete case we used $d^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(i/d)^k\le s^{k+1}/(k+1)$; in the Poisson case this is the exact integral of the monomial.

<a id="loc:refinedincrement"></a>
<a id="loc:etaimproved"></a>
The sharper $y$ inequality improves the sign information. Independence gives $$d\mathbb EW=\mu\eta_j-a\eta\mu_j,\qquad
 d\mathbb E(y_0B_0)=-a\eta\eta_j,\qquad
 d\mathbb ES=\mu\nu_j+a^2\nu\mu_j.$$ Thus the normalized $y$ excess is bounded below and above by $$\label{loc:refinedincrement}
 (6\mu+6a\eta+c a^2\nu)\mu_j
 +(-6\mu-a\eta)\eta_j+c\mu\nu_j,
 \qquad c=-9.5,\ 1,$$ respectively. The coefficient of $\eta_j$ is negative, since $6\mu+a\eta\ge(6-.061m_0)\mu>0$. For the lower estimate the coefficient of $\mu_j$ is positive, since $$6\mu+6a\eta-9.5a^2\nu
 \ge[6-(6(.061)+9.5(1.235))(.05)]\mu
 =5.395075\mu>0.$$ These observations determine the valid directions of all substitutions. Adding the standalone increment and averaging, define $R=(1+a)\eta/(\alpha\mu^2)$. Equations <a href="#loc:partialmu" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:partialmu">[loc:partialmu]</a>–<a href="#loc:partialeta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:partialeta">[loc:partialeta]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 R&\le6.689(.57)+(6+.105)(.05)(2.09/2+.181/3)
       +.05(1.235/2+3.705/3)\\
  &=\frac{2121379}{500000}<4.25,\\
 R&\ge[6-(6(.061)+9.5(1.235))(.05)](1/2-.05/3)\\
 &\hspace{1em}-(6+.105)(.05)(.061/2+6.27/3)
       -9.5(.05)(1.235/2+3.705/3)\\
  &=\frac{25929587}{24000000}>0.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $6+(6(2.09)+1.235)(.05)=6.68875<6.689$, $2.09(.05)=.1045<.105$, and $1.14/2=.57$. Consequently <a id="loc:etapositive"></a>$$\label{loc:etapositive}
 \eta\ge0,\qquad 0\le\frac{a\eta}{\mu^2}
       \le\frac{4.25}{3}<1.417.$$ The standalone $y$ increment is now nonpositive. Keeping the preceding coarse partial bounds in the upper expression of <a href="#loc:refinedincrement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:refinedincrement">[loc:refinedincrement]</a>, its coefficients of $s$ and $s^2$ after division by $\mu^2$ are at most $$6.689(1.14)+(6+.105)(.05)(2.09)+.05(1.235)
   =8.3251825<8.34,$$ $$(6+.105)(.05)(.181)+.05(3.705)=.24050025<.242.$$ Summation or integration therefore improves the partial bound to $$\label{loc:etaimproved}
 \eta_j/\mu^2\le4.17s^2+.081s^3,$$ since $8.34/2=4.17$ and $.242/3<.081$.

<a id="loc:finalincrement"></a>
The sign information now lets us estimate the average pair excess in $x$. Taking the marginal expectation in Equation <a href="#loc:xpair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:xpair">[loc:xpair]</a> and multiplying by $d$ gives the upper bound $$-\mu\mu_j-2(\mu\eta_j-a\eta\mu_j)-2a\eta\eta_j
       +2.55(\mu\nu_j+a^2\nu\mu_j).$$ The terms $-2\mu\eta_j+C\mu\nu_j$ suggest keeping their combination together. Define $$K_j=C\nu_j-2\eta_j,\qquad G=2a\eta+C a^2\nu.$$ Using $2.55=C+.05$, an exact rearrangement of the bound gives $$\label{loc:finalincrement}
 (-\mu+G)\mu_j+(\mu+a\eta)K_j-Ca\eta\nu_j
       +.05(\mu\nu_j+a^2\nu\mu_j).$$ The term $-Ca\eta\nu_j$ is nonpositive. The remaining new quantity is the average of $K_j$, which we estimate before returning to the $x$ excess.

<a id="loc:G"></a>
The standalone normalized $K$ increment is $G$, with $$\label{loc:G}
 0\le G\le[2(1.417)+2.5(1.235)]\mu^2
       =5.9215\mu^2<5.922\mu^2.$$ Equation <a href="#loc:kpair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:kpair">[loc:kpair]</a> bounds its normalized excess by $$(-2\mu-22a\eta+55.5a^2\nu)\mu_j
 +(22\mu+7a\eta)\eta_j+26\mu\nu_j.$$ After discarding $-22a\eta\le0$, the coefficient of $\mu_j$ divided by $\mu$ is at most $-2+55.5(1.235)(.05)=1.427125<1.43$. The coefficient of $\eta_j$ is positive, allowing use of <a href="#loc:etaimproved" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:etaimproved">[loc:etaimproved]</a>.

<a id="loc:Kaverage"></a>
The average of a partial sum equals the weighted average of its increments. More precisely, in the regular case, for $w_j=(d-1-j)/d$, $$\operatorname{av}K_j
 =\operatorname{av}\{w_j d(K_{j+1}-K_j)\}.$$ In the Poisson case Fubini gives the same identity with weight $1-s$ and derivative in $s$. Direct finite sums, or the corresponding integrals, give <a id="loc:weightedaverages"></a>$$\label{loc:weightedaverages}
 \operatorname{av}w=\frac\alpha2,\quad
 \operatorname{av}(ws)\le\frac\alpha6,\quad
 \operatorname{av}(ws^2)\le\frac\alpha{12}.$$ The latter two discrete values are $(d-2)(d-1)/(6d^2)$ and $(d-2)(d-1)^2/(12d^3)$, respectively. Since $s^3\le s^2$, applying <a href="#loc:partialmu" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:partialmu">[loc:partialmu]</a>, <a href="#loc:partialnu" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:partialnu">[loc:partialnu]</a>, <a href="#loc:etaimproved" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:etaimproved">[loc:etaimproved]</a>, and <a href="#loc:G" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:G">[loc:G]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\operatorname{av}K_j}{\alpha\mu^2}
 &\le5.922/2+1.43(1.14)/6\nonumber\\
 &\quad+(22+7(1.417)(.05))(.05)(4.17+.081)/12\nonumber\\
 &\quad+26(.05)(1.235/6+3.705/12)\nonumber\\
 &=\frac{20640565669}{4800000000}<4.31.
 \label{loc:Kaverage}
\end{aligned}$$ No nonnegativity of $K_j$ has been assumed.

We can now average Equation <a href="#loc:finalincrement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:finalincrement">[loc:finalincrement]</a>. The third term is nonpositive. Use the lower bound on $\mu_j$ for $-\mu\mu_j$ and the upper bound for $G\mu_j$, where $G\ge0$. For the $K_j$ term first use <a href="#loc:Kaverage" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:Kaverage">[loc:Kaverage]</a>, since $\mu+a\eta>0$, and then bound its positive coefficient. After division by $\alpha\mu^2>0$, the average of <a href="#loc:finalincrement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:finalincrement">[loc:finalincrement]</a> is at most $$\begin{aligned}
 &-(1/2-.05/3)+5.922(.05)(.57)
       +(1+1.417(.05))(.05)(4.31)\\
 &\hspace{2em}+.05(.05)(1.235/2+3.705/3+1.235(.57))
       =-\frac{2321911}{30000000}<0.
\end{aligned}$$ This contradicts the zero actual excess average and proves the proposition. The omitted case $\mu=0$ is exactly the uniform posterior experiment. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:weighted"></a>
### Negative equality: a global weighted logarithm inequality

The local result leaves only fixed points with $\mu>1/20$. We now exclude these except in a short interval near the coloring channel. The scalar inequality in this section is certified by the integer interval calculation in Appendix <a href="#int:appendix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="int:appendix">[int:appendix]</a>, with its complete source in Section <a href="#cert:interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:interval">11.2</a>.

<a id="stmt-wei-exclusion"></a>
<div id="wei:exclusion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Global exclusion away from coloring). *<span id="wei:theorem" label="wei:theorem"></span>At $\lambda=-a$, $d=a^{-2}$, the uniform posterior law is the only permutation-invariant fixed point in either of the following ranges:*

1.  *regular offspring, with integer $d$ and $0<a\le.448$;*

2.  *Poisson offspring, with $0<a\le.477$.*

*In particular the regular assertion covers every integer $d\ge5$.*

</div>

For a positive likelihood vector $m=1+v$ define <a id="wei:functions"></a>$$\label{wei:functions}
 G=(m_0m_1m_2)^{1/3},\quad T=1-G,\quad
 u_i=\log m_i-\langle \log m\rangle,\quad
 W=G\langle u^2\rangle,\quad Y=G\langle u^3\rangle.$$ Here $\langle \cdot\rangle$ is the average of the three coordinates. Edge subscripts mean evaluation on $1-av$, and we use $$\widehat T=dT_e,\qquad\widehat W=dW_e,\qquad\widehat Y=dY_e.$$ The functions $T,W,Y$ extend continuously to the closed simplex, with $T=1$ and $W=Y=0$ whenever any coordinate vanishes. Indeed $G$ times any fixed power of the logarithms tends to zero as a coordinate vanishes: for example the product $\prod_i m_i^{1/3}$ times $\sum_i|\log m_i|^3$ is bounded by a sum of terms having a factor $m_i^{1/3}|\log m_i|^3\to0$ and the other coordinates are bounded by three. This observation also proves boundedness of these extensions.

<a id="stmt-wei-identities"></a>
<div id="wei:identities" class="lemma">

<a id="wei:exact"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Weighted fixed-point identities). *Let $0<a<1/2$, $d=a^{-2}$, and let the fixed point be nontrivial. Set <a id="wei:scalars"></a>$$\label{wei:scalars}
 \Phi(t)=\mathbb Et^{D},\qquad A=\mathbb EG_e,\qquad
 z=d(1-A),\qquad h=\frac{1-\Phi(A)}{d(1-A)},\qquad
 1-\xi=\frac{\Phi'(A)}d.$$ Then $0<A<1$, $0<\xi<1$, and $$\label{wei:exact}
 \mathbb ET=h\mathbb E\widehat T,\qquad
 \mathbb EW=(1-\xi)\mathbb E\widehat W,\qquad
 \mathbb EY=(1-\xi)\mathbb E\widehat Y,\qquad
 \mathbb E\widehat T=z.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-wei-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-wei-identities</summary>

*Proof.* Edge likelihood coordinates lie in $[1-2a,1+a]$, so $G_e>0$. Their coordinate average is one, hence $G_e\le1$, with strict inequality on nonuniform messages. Nontriviality and $a>0$ thus give $0<A<1$. The assertions about $\xi$ follow from $\Phi'(A)/d=A^{d-1}$ in the regular case and $\Phi'(A)/d=e^{d(A-1)}$ in the Poisson case.

For $n$ branches let $Z=\langle \prod_{j=1}^n m_{e,j}\rangle$ be the product likelihood normalizer, and let $g_j=G_{e,j}$. Under the independent marginal branch law the actual output law is tilted by $Z$, whereas its geometric mean equals $\prod_jg_j/Z$. Their product is $\prod_jg_j$. Centered log coordinates add, $u_{\mathrm{out},i}=\sum_j u_{e,j,i}$, because normalization changes all three logs by the same constant. For each fixed coordinate $i$, permutation invariance and centering give $$\mathbb E(G_e u_{e,i})=0:$$ the three expectations are equal and their sum is zero. Consequently every cross term in the square or cube of $\sum_j u_{e,j,i}$ has a vanishing first-moment factor. Independence therefore proves, for $n\ge1$, $$\mathbb E(G_{\mathrm{out}}\mid n)=A^n,\quad
 \mathbb E(W_{\mathrm{out}}\mid n)=nA^{n-1}\mathbb EW_e,\quad
 \mathbb E(Y_{\mathrm{out}}\mid n)=nA^{n-1}\mathbb EY_e.$$ At $n=0$ the output is uniform, so the contributions to $T,W,Y$ are zero. Mixing over $D$ gives <a href="#wei:exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:exact">[wei:exact]</a>. All terms are integrable: the edge logs are uniformly bounded and the output centered logs grow at most linearly in $n$, while both offspring laws have finite moments of every fixed order. No conditioning on survival enters these identities. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-wei-scalarbounds"></a>
<div id="wei:scalarbounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Scalar bounds). *In the regular range of Proposition <a href="#wei:exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:exclusion">16</a>, or in its Poisson range, one can use constants $$(H,U)=\left(\frac1{16},\frac{15}{32}\right)$$ to obtain <a id="wei:hz"></a>$$\label{wei:hz}
 h\le\overline h:=1-\frac\xi2-H\xi^2-\frac H2\xi^3,
 \qquad z\ge\underline z:=\xi+U\xi^2.$$ For Poisson offspring the stronger values $(H,U)=(1/12,1/2)$ also hold. Every nontrivial fixed point in the claimed ranges satisfies $.03<\xi<1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-wei-scalarbounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-wei-scalarbounds</summary>

*Proof.* Regular offspring in the stated range have integer $d\ge5$. Since $1-\xi=A^{d-1}$, the finite geometric sum gives $$h=\frac1d\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}(1-\xi)^{i/(d-1)}.$$ For Poisson offspring $1-\xi=e^{-z}$ and $$h=\frac{1-e^{-z}}z=\int_0^1(1-\xi)^t\,dt.$$ In either expression the averaging law of $t$ is symmetric under $t\mapsto1-t$, so $\mathbb E_t t=1/2$. For $0\le t\le1$ all nonconstant coefficients in the binomial expansion of $(1-\xi)^t$ are nonpositive. The first three negative coefficients, after averaging, are $$\frac12,\qquad \frac{\mathbb E_t[t(1-t)]}{2},\qquad
 \frac{\mathbb E_t[t(1-t)(2-t)]}{6}
       =\frac{\mathbb E_t[t(1-t)]}{4}.$$ The last equality uses symmetry, since the $t(1-t)$-weighted mean of $t$ is $1/2$. In the regular case $$\mathbb E_t[t(1-t)]=\frac{d-2}{6(d-1)}\ge\frac18;$$ in the Poisson case it is $1/6$. Truncating after degree three gives the bound for $h$. It holds at $\xi=1$ as well by continuity.

For regular offspring, $$z=d\{1-(1-\xi)^{1/(d-1)}\}.$$ All coefficients of this series are nonnegative. Its linear coefficient is $d/(d-1)\ge1$, and its quadratic coefficient obeys $$\frac{d(d-2)}{2(d-1)^2}-\frac{15}{32}
       =\frac{(d-5)(d+3)}{32(d-1)^2}\ge0.$$ This proves the claimed lower bound. For Poisson offspring $z=-\log(1-\xi)\ge\xi+\xi^2/2$; the weaker values of $H,U$ are consequently valid there too.

Finally $G_e^3=1-3a^2x-2a^3y$, so, since $d=a^{-2}$, <a id="wei:That"></a>$$\label{wei:That}
 \widehat T=\frac{3x+2ay}{1+G_e+G_e^2}
       \ge(1-a/3)x.$$ For this inequality use $G_e\le1$ and $y\ge-x/2$ from <a href="#loc:ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:ratio">[loc:ratio]</a>; the numerator is nonnegative. By Proposition <a href="#loc:gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:gap">14</a> and $a\le.477$, $$z=\mathbb E\widehat T>(1-.477/3)/20=.04205>.042.$$ For regular $d\ge5$, $$\log(1-\xi)=(d-1)\log(1-z/d)\le-(1-1/d)z\le-.8z;$$ for Poisson, $1-\xi=e^{-z}\le e^{-.8z}$. Since $1-e^{-t}\ge t/(1+t)$ for $t\ge0$, in either case $$\xi>\frac{.0336}{1+.0336}=\frac{21}{646}>.0325>.03.$$ This verifies the complete $\xi$ domain required below. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### A pointwise inequality from exact identities

<a id="wei:zero-mean"></a>
The identities above give several functions with expectation zero. We combine them so that the scalar bounds on $h$ and $z$ produce a function that is strictly positive at every message. For a nontrivial fixed point, choose signed rational constants $c_0,c_1,s_1$ and a positive rational $\delta$, and put $$s_0=c_0+1/3,\qquad c(\xi)=c_0+c_1\xi,\qquad
 s(\xi)=s_0+s_1\xi.$$ The relation defining $s_0$ removes the cubic term of $T+c_0W+s_0Y$ near the uniform message. Indeed, expansion at $v=0$ gives $$T=x-\tfrac23y+O(\|v\|^4),\qquad
 W=2x-2y+O(\|v\|^4),\qquad Y=2y+O(\|v\|^4).$$ Its quadratic term also cancels on subtracting $d$ times its edge evaluation, since $da^2=1$. By Lemma <a href="#wei:identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:identities">17</a>, the random expression $$\begin{aligned}
 E_0={}&T-h\widehat T
       +c(\xi)[W-(1-\xi)\widehat W]
       +s(\xi)[Y-(1-\xi)\widehat Y]\nonumber\\
     &+\delta\xi(z-\widehat T)
 \label{wei:zero-mean}
\end{aligned}$$ has expectation zero. In particular, the final term changes the pointwise expression without changing its expectation, since $\mathbb E\widehat T=z$.

<a id="wei:comparison"></a>
Fix an applicable pair $(H,U)$ from Lemma <a href="#wei:scalarbounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:scalarbounds">18</a>. Replace $h$ in <a href="#wei:zero-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:zero-mean">[wei:zero-mean]</a> by $\overline h=1-\xi/2-H\xi^2-(H/2)\xi^3$ and $z$ by $\underline z=\xi+U\xi^2$, and call the resulting expression $P$. The only changes are in terms whose signs are controlled: $$\label{wei:comparison}
 P-E_0=(h-\overline h)\widehat T
          +\delta\xi(\underline z-z)\le0.$$ Here $\widehat T\ge0$, $\delta>0$, and $\xi\ge0$. The possibly negative coefficients $c(\xi)$ and $s(\xi)$ multiply identities that remain exact. Thus $\mathbb EP\le0$ for any nontrivial fixed point; it remains to choose the constants so that $P>0$ pointwise.

<a id="wei:polynomial"></a>
<a id="wei:coefficients"></a>
<a id="wei:parameters"></a>
Expanding the relaxed expression gives $$\label{wei:polynomial}
 P=D_0+D_1\xi+D_2\xi^2+D_3\xi^3,$$ where $$\begin{aligned}
 D_0={}&T-\widehat T+c_0(W-\widehat W)+s_0(Y-\widehat Y),\nonumber\\
 D_1={}&c_1(W-\widehat W)+s_1(Y-\widehat Y)
       +c_0\widehat W+s_0\widehat Y+(1/2-\delta)\widehat T,\nonumber\\
 D_2={}&c_1\widehat W+s_1\widehat Y+H\widehat T+\delta,\nonumber\\
 D_3={}&U\delta+(H/2)\widehat T.
 \label{wei:coefficients}
\end{aligned}$$ We use the following rational choices on three closed $a$ bands: $$\label{wei:parameters}
\begin{array}{c|rrrr|cc}
 a\text{ band}&1000c_0&1000c_1&1000s_1&1000\delta&H&U\\ \hline
 \bigl[0,.190\bigr]&-268&-60&-55&130&1/16&15/32\\
 \bigl[.190,.448\bigr]&-269&67&47&55&1/16&15/32\\
 \bigl[.448,.477\bigr]&-271&58&49&50&1/12&1/2
\end{array}$$ The final row uses the stronger scalar bounds for Poisson offspring. Overlapping endpoints can use either applicable row. For purposes of the pointwise inequality, extend the hatted functions to $a=0$ by $$\widehat T=x,\qquad\widehat W=2x,\qquad\widehat Y=0.$$ These are their continuous limits: use $G_e^3=1-3a^2x-2a^3y$ for $\widehat T$ and $\log(1-av_i)=-av_i+O(a^2)$ for the centered edge logarithms.

<a id="stmt-wei-positive"></a>
<div id="wei:positive" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Certified global inequality). *For each row of <a href="#wei:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:parameters">[wei:parameters]</a>, every posterior probability vector, every $a$ in that row’s closed band, and every $.03\le\xi\le1$, the polynomial <a href="#wei:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:polynomial">[wei:polynomial]</a> satisfies $P>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-wei-positive"><summary>Proof of stmt-wei-positive</summary>

*Proof.* Appendix <a href="#int:appendix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="int:appendix">[int:appendix]</a> proves the exact interval certificate for this inequality, with the complete source in Section <a href="#cert:interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:interval">11.2</a>. It parametrizes sorted probability vectors as $(1,q^{18},r^{18})/(1+q^{18}+r^{18})$, where $0\le r\le q\le1$. In these coordinates the weighted logarithmic functions have continuous first derivatives at zero coordinates. The appendix derives formulas for the hatted functions without division by $a$, so their values and first derivatives are enclosed also at $a=0$. It then identifies the four expressions computed by the program with $D_0,D_1,D_2,D_3$ above.

The certificate covers the full $(q,r,a)$ domain by closed boxes and partitions $\xi\in[.03,1]$ at $$.03,.05,.075,.10,.15,.20,.30,.40,.50,.65,.80,.90,1.$$ On every resulting interval it certifies strictly positive lower bounds for the four cubic Bernstein coefficients. All enclosures, including the derivative bounds used for midpoint acceptance and subdivision, follow from integer arithmetic and explicit analytic remainder bounds. The appendix proves coverage of every simplex boundary and band endpoint and records normal completion of all three prescribed runs with assertions enabled. Positive Bernstein coefficients imply $P>0$ throughout each closed $\xi$ interval. At the uniform message this also follows directly from $P=\delta\xi^2+U\delta\xi^3>0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-wei-positive-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-wei-positive</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#wei:exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:exclusion">16</a>.* Suppose a nontrivial fixed point exists. Lemmas <a href="#wei:identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:identities">17</a> and <a href="#wei:scalarbounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:scalarbounds">18</a> place its scalar $\xi$ in the certified interval. Choose the applicable row of <a href="#wei:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:parameters">[wei:parameters]</a>. Equation <a href="#wei:zero-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:zero-mean">[wei:zero-mean]</a> has expectation zero, so <a href="#wei:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:comparison">[wei:comparison]</a> gives $\mathbb EP\le0$. On the other hand, Lemma <a href="#wei:positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:positive">19</a> gives $P>0$ pointwise. The functions in $P$ are bounded on the closed simplex for the fixed $a<1/2$, so $\mathbb EP>0$, a contradiction.

For the final assertion, $a=1/\sqrt d\le1/\sqrt5<.448$ whenever $d\ge5$: the strict comparison follows from $5(.448)^2=1.00352>1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="grid:section"></a>
### A finite certificate at the coloring boundary

We now prove non-reconstruction at the remaining negative equalities, including the channel $\lambda=-1/2$. Throughout this section $a=-\lambda>0$, $p=(p_0,p_1,p_2)$ is a posterior probability vector, and $\zeta=\sum_{i=0}^2p_i\omega^i$, where $\omega=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}$. In this section the input spin $S$ denotes its index in $\{0,1,2\}$; its complex representative is $\omega^S$. Write $\mu=\mathbb E|\zeta|^2$. Proposition <a href="#loc:gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:gap">14</a> says that a symmetric fixed point at $da^2=1$ with $\mu\leq 1/20$ is the uniform posterior. It will therefore suffice to dominate one finite-depth experiment by an explicit experiment with $\mu<1/20$. The dominating process need not itself be at equality or converge to the uniform posterior.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Explicit refinements of posterior experiments

The finite representation follows the survey method of Bhatnagar and Maneva (Bhatnagar and Maneva 2011, secs. 3.1–3.3 and Theorems 5–8) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[BhatnagarManeva2011](#ref-BhatnagarManeva2011)]: posterior vectors are replaced by barycentric mixtures on a finite grid, and rounding deficits and omitted offspring tails are completed by perfectly informative messages. We give explicit refinement kernels for every operation and exact arithmetic certificates for the three-state parameter ranges used here.

<a id="grid:canonical"></a>
For experiments with the same input spin $S$, write $\mathcal E\preceq
\mathcal F$ if a Markov kernel, independent of $S$, transforms the data of $\mathcal F$ into data with the conditional laws of $\mathcal E$. Such a kernel is called a garbling. Under the uniform prior, an experiment with datum $Y$, marginal law $\rho$, and posterior $p(Y)$ satisfies $$\mathbb P(S=i,Y\in dy)=p_i(y)\rho(dy),\qquad
 \mathbb P(Y\in dy\mid S=i)=3p_i(y)\rho(dy).
 \label{grid:canonical}$$ Its full labeled posterior is an equivalent experiment: the reverse kernel from $p$ to $Y$ is the conditional marginal law $\rho(dy\mid p(Y)=p)$. Equation <a href="#grid:canonical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:canonical">[grid:canonical]</a> proves that this kernel also works under every input spin. All conditional distributions used here exist on the finite or standard Borel spaces in question.

We compress a permutation-invariant posterior law by storing one mass for each orbit under the six coordinate permutations. The represented datum is always the *full labeled posterior*. Under its marginal law, draw an orbit, uniformly permute its representative, and retain the resulting vector. Repeated coordinates cause no difficulty, since each distinct orientation has the same multiplicity among the six permutations. Observing only the orbit index would be a different experiment and is not used below.

<a id="stmt-grid-spread"></a>
<div id="grid:spread" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Barycentric refinement). *Suppose that for every old datum $y$ there are probabilities $\alpha_y(z)$ on a finite set of posterior vectors such that $\sum_z\alpha_y(z)z=p(y)$. Then the posterior law $\nu(z)=\int\alpha_y(z)\rho(dy)$ defines an experiment that dominates the old one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-grid-spread"><summary>Proof of stmt-grid-spread</summary>

<a id="grid:spread-forward"></a>
*Proof.* Here is an explicit refinement conditional on the input spin. Given $S=i$ and $Y=y$ with $p_i(y)>0$, choose $z$ with probability $$R_i(y,z)=\frac{\alpha_y(z)z_i}{p_i(y)}.
 \label{grid:spread-forward}$$ Choose any distribution on the null event $p_i(y)=0$. The barycenter identity makes this a probability kernel. Its joint law is $\mathbb P(S=i,Y\in dy,Z=z)=\rho(dy)\alpha_y(z)z_i$, so that the posterior given $(Y,Z)$ is $Z$. After retaining just $Z$, the reverse garbling is <a id="grid:spread-reverse"></a>$$G(z,dy)=\frac{\rho(dy)\alpha_y(z)}{\nu(z)}\quad\text{if }\nu(z)>0.
 \label{grid:spread-reverse}$$ It is independent of $i$ and recovers the old conditional law, since $\sum_z3z_i\nu(z)G(z,dy)=3p_i(y)\rho(dy)$. Arbitrary kernels at $\nu(z)=0$ are harmless. In particular $\alpha_y$ specifies *marginal* spreading probabilities; the actual refinement conditional on the spin is <a href="#grid:spread-forward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:spread-forward">[grid:spread-forward]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-grid-completion"></a>
<div id="grid:completion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Symmetric completion by pure posteriors). *Let a finite symmetric posterior law have orbit masses $W_o$, and suppose $0\leq L_o\leq W_o$ for each orbit. The law retaining masses $L_o$ and putting the remaining mass $1-\sum_oL_o$ at the uniformly permuted pure orbit dominates the original experiment.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-grid-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-grid-completion</summary>

*Proof.* On a datum $p$ in orbit $o$, retain $p$ with probability $t(p)=L_o/W_o$ (put $t=0$ when $W_o=0$). Otherwise reveal the input spin. More precisely, conditional on $(S=i,p)$ the refined datum is $(p,0)$ with probability $t(p)$ and $(p,1,i)$ otherwise. Forgetting the flag and the revealed spin recovers $p$, so this is a refinement. The respective posteriors are $p$ and $e_i$, and their full posterior is sufficient by <a href="#grid:canonical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:canonical">[grid:canonical]</a>. Removal is constant across each orbit, whence $$\mathbb P(S=i,\text{removed from }o)
   =(W_o-L_o)\frac16\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak S_3}(\pi p)_i
   =\frac{W_o-L_o}{3}.$$ Thus the revealed part is exactly the stated pure orbit, with no change to the uniform input prior. This also covers removal from the pure orbit: retaining a pure posterior and revealing the already known spin are disjoint flagged outcomes that compress to the same posterior, so their masses add. For completeness, if $\rho(p)$ denotes an old labeled posterior mass and $\nu(z)$ the resulting mass, the reverse kernel after posterior compression is explicitly $$G(z,p)=\frac{\rho(p)}{\nu(z)}
 \left[t(p)\mathbf1_{\{z=p\}}+
 (1-t(p))\sum_{i=0}^2p_i\mathbf1_{\{z=e_i\}}\right]
 \quad(\nu(z)>0).\qedhere$$ ◻

</details>

Both independent composition and edge transmission preserve $\preceq$. In detail, if $G_j$ garbles the $j$th upper branch into the $j$th lower branch, instantiate the upper branch data independently *conditional on their common input spin*, and use the product kernel $\prod_jG_j$ with independent auxiliary randomness. Under each input this recovers the product of the lower conditional laws. For an edge, averaging these same conditional identities against the channel $K_\lambda(i,j)$ proves the claim. Mixing over an input-independent count also preserves the order: retain the count and apply the appropriate product garbling conditional on it. These facts justify each induction below; no monotonicity of the numerical rounding map is assumed.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Grid spreading and exact product tables

Fix $$H=72,\qquad N=10^{12},\qquad
 \mathcal H=\{(h_0,h_1,h_2)\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0}^3:
     h_0\geq h_1\geq h_2,\ h_0+h_1+h_2=H\}.$$ There are $469$ representatives in $\mathcal H$. An integer vector of nonnegative masses $(m_h)_{h\in\mathcal H}$ summing to $N$ represents the symmetric posterior law with orbit probabilities $m_h/N$ and posteriors $h/H$. Denote the pure and uniform orbit laws by $\mathsf P$ and $\mathsf U$.

For an integer vector $r\geq0$ of positive total $T$, set $$n_i=\left\lfloor\frac{Hr_i}{T}\right\rfloor,\qquad
 b_i=Hr_i-n_iT,\qquad
 k=H-\sum_i n_i=\frac{\sum_i b_i}{T}\in\{0,1,2\}.$$ If $k=0$, choose $n/H$. If $k=1$, choose $(n+e_i)/H$ with probability $b_i/T$. If $k=2$, choose $(n+\mathbf1-e_i)/H$ with probability $(T-b_i)/T$. These are valid grid vectors and probabilities. For $k=2$ the expected increment in coordinate $j$ is $\sum_{i\ne j}(1-b_i/T)=b_j/T$; the other two cases are immediate. The chosen vector therefore has mean $r/T$, and Lemma <a href="#grid:spread" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:spread">20</a> proves refinement. The construction commutes with label permutations.

<a id="grid:modes"></a>
For rational edge magnitude $a=A/B\leq1/2$, first reduce $A/B$ to lowest terms. A child grid posterior $q/H$ gives parent likelihood entries $$\frac{e_i(q)}{BH},\qquad e_i(q)=(B+A)H-3Aq_i,$$ whereas its plain likelihood entries are $3q_i/H$. To combine two orbit representatives $p,q$, fix the orientation of $p$ and average the six relative permutations $\pi$ of $q$. This is sufficient because a simultaneous permutation changes only the output orientation, which remains uniform within its orbit. The following table specifies the three operations used by the verifier: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 \text{mode}&\text{operation}&r_i&D_{\mathrm{tab}}\\ \hline
 0&\text{plain--plain}&p_i\,3q_{\pi(i)}&6H^2\\
 1&\text{plain--edge}&p_i\,e_{\pi(i)}(q)&6BH^2\\
 2&\text{edge--edge}&e_i(p)e_{\pi(i)}(q)&18B^2H^2
\end{array}
\label{grid:modes}$$ In each case, with $T=\sum_i r_i$, the posterior is $r/T$ and the relative permutation’s probability after the likelihood tilt is $T/D_{\mathrm{tab}}$. For example the mode 1 tilt is $\frac13\sum_i(3p_i/H)(e_{\pi(i)}(q)/(BH))=T/(BH^2)$; the additional permutation factor is $1/6$. The other rows follow in the same way. A permutation with $T=0$ has zero probability and contributes nothing, including at $a=1/2$.

<a id="grid:table-mass"></a>
Multiply these probabilities by the grid spread. The resulting table numerators are respectively $T$ when $k=0$, $b_i$ when $k=1$, and $T-b_i$ when $k=2$, all over the row’s common denominator $D_{\mathrm{tab}}$. Aggregate contributions reaching the same output orbit. Write the integer result as $w_{p,q}(h)$. For every pair, $$w_{p,q}(h)\geq0,\qquad \sum_h w_{p,q}(h)=D_{\mathrm{tab}}.
 \label{grid:table-mass}$$ Indeed the spread has mass one, and averaging either input likelihood over uniform label permutations gives one in each coordinate. The verifier additionally asserts <a href="#grid:table-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:table-mass">[grid:table-mass]</a> for every entry.

<a id="grid:combine"></a>
For input integer mass laws $m,n$, keep output units $$\ell_h=\left\lfloor
  \frac{\sum_{p,q}m_pn_qw_{p,q}(h)}{N D_{\mathrm{tab}}}
 \right\rfloor,
 \label{grid:combine}$$ and add $N-\sum_h\ell_h$ units to the pure orbit. The unfloored quantity divided by $N$ is the exact orbit mass of the spread product, so Lemma <a href="#grid:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:completion">21</a> proves that this operation dominates the indicated exact product. Denote it by $\mathcal C_0,\mathcal C_1$ or $\mathcal C_2$. Modes 0 and 2 have symmetric tables in $p,q$: exchange the inputs, invert the relative permutation, and relabel the output. Thus their code uses pairs $p\leq q$ with diagonal coefficient $m_pn_p$ and off-diagonal coefficient $m_pn_q+m_qn_p$. Mode 1 uses all ordered pairs.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Regular and Poisson upper recursions

<a id="grid:regular-recursion"></a>
For the regular tree with four children at each vertex at $a=1/2$, start at $m^{(0)}=\mathsf P$ and set $$h^{(t)}=\mathcal C_2(m^{(t)},m^{(t)}),\qquad
 m^{(t+1)}=\mathcal C_0(h^{(t)},h^{(t)}).
 \label{grid:regular-recursion}$$ The two copies of $h^{(t)}$ are independent conditional on the root; each dominates the product of two edge-transformed upper child experiments. The refinement and composition arguments give, by induction, domination of the exact four-branch depth-$t$ experiment.

<a id="grid:mixture"></a>
For a Poisson tree of chosen mean $d_+$ and rational magnitude $a_+$, fix an upper child law $m$. Its count-specific upper laws are <a id="grid:counts"></a>$$Q_0=\mathsf U,\qquad Q_n=\mathcal C_1(Q_{n-1},m).
 \label{grid:counts}$$ Each $Q_n$ dominates the exact $n$-branch experiment, with the child law $m$ held fixed throughout this count recursion. Write $q_n(h)$ for its integer units. If $p_n=e^{-d_+}d_+^n/n!$ and $w_n$ are integer lower weights satisfying $0\leq w_n/N\leq p_n$, form $$\ell_h=\sum_{n\text{ retained}}
          \left\lfloor\frac{w_nq_n(h)}N\right\rfloor
 \label{grid:mixture}$$ and complete the mass to $N$ at the pure orbit.

To justify the count and tail operations, first draw the count with its *exact* probabilities $p_n$, then the upper experiment $Q_n$ conditional on that count and the common input spin. For omitted counts one may take $Q_n=\mathsf P$, which dominates every experiment: given a revealed spin, simulate the required conditional data. With count included as data the resulting mixture dominates the exact Poisson experiment. For each count $n$, the upper law is a calibrated posterior experiment: if $\rho_n$ is its labeled marginal law, then its conditional law under input $i$ is $3p_i\rho_n(dp)$, as in <a href="#grid:canonical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:canonical">[grid:canonical]</a>. The product tilt, barycentric refinement, and pure completion all preserve this identity, including the chosen pure law at omitted counts. Since the count has input-independent probabilities $p_n$, its joint law with the input and posterior is $\mathbb P(S=i,n,p\in dp)=p_n p_i\rho_n(dp)$. Consequently the posterior given $(n,p)$ is $p$. Posterior sufficiency therefore allows count to be forgotten while preserving equivalence. Its orbit masses are $W_h=\sum_n p_nq_n(h)/N$, interpreting $q_n$ as the chosen pure law on omitted counts. Equation <a href="#grid:mixture" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:mixture">[grid:mixture]</a> satisfies $\ell_h/N\leq W_h$ for every orbit. Lemma <a href="#grid:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:completion">21</a> applies to this one exact mixture, including the tail and all rounding losses. Thus iterating this completed Poisson operation from $\mathsf P$ gives upper laws at every depth.

<a id="grid:taylor"></a>
Here the weights are constructed entirely with integers. Put $S_0=10^{15}$ and $d_+=d_{\max}/200\leq881/200$. Beginning with $l_0=u_0=S_0$, compute for $1\leq n\leq41$ $$l_n=\left\lfloor\frac{l_{n-1}d_{\max}}{200n}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad
 u_n=\left\lceil\frac{u_{n-1}d_{\max}}{200n}\right\rceil.$$ These enclose $S_0d_+^n/n!$. The degree-41 Taylor polynomial of $e^{-d_+}$ is a lower bound, since its degree-42 Lagrange remainder is positive. Hence, with $$L=S_0+\sum_{\substack{1\leq n\leq41\\n\text{ even}}}l_n
             -\sum_{\substack{1\leq n\leq41\\n\text{ odd}}}u_n,
 \qquad
 w_0=\left\lfloor\frac{NL}{S_0}\right\rfloor,
 \label{grid:taylor}$$ we have $w_0/N\leq e^{-d_+}$. Positivity of $L$, and all arithmetic bounds, are proved in Appendix <a href="#grid:implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:implementation">10</a>. Continue with $w_n=\lfloor w_{n-1}d_{\max}/(200n)\rfloor$ until the first zero. Induction gives $w_n/N\leq p_n$. For $n\geq9$ one has $d_+/n<1/2$, so the positive integer weights eventually vanish.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Closed parameter coverage and certificate outputs

<a id="grid:bins"></a>
<a id="grid:magnitude"></a>
For $j=0,\ldots,26$ set $$d_{\min}=800+3j,\quad d_{\max}=803+3j,\quad
 I_j=[d_{\min}/200,d_{\max}/200],\quad d_+=d_{\max}/200.
 \label{grid:bins}$$ Choose $a_+=A/10^6$, where $A$ is the least positive integer such that $$A^2d_{\min}\geq200\cdot10^{12}.
 \label{grid:magnitude}$$ The integer binary search in the verifier finds this $A$ without square roots. Its upper endpoint $500000$ is valid since $d_{\min}\geq800$, so $a_+\leq1/2$ is admissible. Minimality also gives $(A-1)^2d_{\min}<200\cdot10^{12}$. For every equality point $d\in I_j$, $a=d^{-1/2}$, we have $d\leq d_+$ and $a\leq a_+$.

By Proposition <a href="#fnd:degradation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fnd:degradation">10</a>, these coordinatewise bounds imply that, at every finite depth, the equality experiment with parameters $(d,-a)$ is degraded from the single corner experiment $(d_+,-a_+)$. The two kernels use thinning probability $d/d_+$ and channel factor $c=a/a_+\in[0,1]$, respectively. They require only the observed tree, its boundary spins, and independent randomness, and remain valid at the closed bin endpoints, including $a_+=1/2$.

<a id="grid:overlap"></a>
The intervals <a href="#grid:bins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:bins">[grid:bins]</a> share endpoints and have union $[4,881/200]$. There is overlap with the analytic Poisson range in Proposition <a href="#wei:exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:exclusion">16</a>, because $$\frac{881}{200}-\frac1{(477/1000)^2}
       =\frac{453049}{45505800}>0.
 \label{grid:overlap}$$ Consequently these rectangles cover every Poisson equality not already covered there. No continuity assertion about the rounded recursion, or sampling of intermediate parameters, is involved. Figure <a href="#fig:dominating-bin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:dominating-bin">1</a> illustrates the domination in the first bin.

<figure id="fig:dominating-bin">

<figcaption>Domination in the first closed Poisson bin $[4,803/200]$; the two axes use different enlarged scales. The upper corner has $a_{+} = 1/2$ and $d_{+} = 803/200$, so it dominates every equality experiment in the bin by thinning offspring and reducing the channel magnitude. It lies above the equality curve. The certificate only brings its finite-depth upper law below $μ = 1/20$; the local fixed-point gap is applied to the target equality experiment.</figcaption>
</figure>
