<a id="grid:moment"></a>
<a id="grid:outputs"></a>
For each grid representative, $$\left|\sum_i\frac{h_i}{H}\omega^i\right|^2
   =1-\frac{3(h_0h_1+h_1h_2+h_2h_0)}{H^2}.$$ Define the integer $$M(m)=\sum_hm_h\bigl[H^2-3(h_0h_1+h_1h_2+h_2h_0)\bigr].
 \label{grid:moment}$$ The certificate stops exactly when $20M(m)<NH^2$, equivalently $\mu(m)<1/20$. The exact code is included in Section <a href="#cert:grid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:grid">11.3</a>. With assertions enabled it returns iteration $162$ for argument $-1$, the regular coloring recursion with four children at each vertex. For arguments $j=0,\ldots,26$, the Poisson recursions return, in order, $$\begin{gathered}
104,103,103,103,102,102,102,102,102,\\
101,101,101,101,101,101,\\
100,100,100,100,100,100,100,\\
99,99,99,99,99.
\end{gathered}
\label{grid:outputs}$$ All comparisons determining these integers use exact integer arithmetic. Appendix <a href="#grid:implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:implementation">10</a> gives the bounds preventing overflow and the correspondence between each code operation and the constructions proved above.

<a id="stmt-grid-negative-completion"></a>
<div id="grid:negative-completion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22** (Completion of the negative threshold). *For both tree models, every admissible negative parameter with $da^2\leq1$ is non-reconstructible (with $d=b$ for a regular tree).*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-grid-negative-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-grid-negative-completion</summary>

*Proof.* Consider first negative equality. For the regular tree with $b=4$, the upper law at depth $162$ has $\mu<1/20$. For every Poisson equality $d\in[4,881/200]$, choose a bin containing $d$ and its stopping depth from <a href="#grid:outputs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:outputs">[grid:outputs]</a>. The corresponding upper law dominates the exact equality depth experiment by the recursion and the two garblings above. Conditional Jensen gives $\mu_{\mathrm{true}}\leq\mu_{\mathrm{upper}}<1/20$ at that depth. By Proposition <a href="#fnd:limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fnd:limit">6</a>, the actual depth laws have a symmetric fixed-point limit and their moments decrease to its moment. The limit therefore has $\mu<1/20$ and is uniform by Proposition <a href="#loc:gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:gap">14</a>. This proves non-reconstruction in the certified range.

Proposition <a href="#fnd:limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fnd:limit">6</a> includes the coloring endpoint: its proof extends the tilted recursion continuously across zero likelihood normalizers and controls the Poisson count tail. Thus the preceding fixed-point inference also applies at $a=1/2$.

Every remaining admissible regular equality has integer $b\geq5$ and $a\leq1/\sqrt5<448/1000$. Every remaining Poisson equality beyond $881/200$ has $a<477/1000$ by <a href="#grid:overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:overlap">[grid:overlap]</a>. Proposition <a href="#wei:exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wei:exclusion">16</a> excludes nonuniform fixed points in these ranges, completing all negative equalities.

For $d\geq4$, every parameter $-d^{-1/2}\le\lambda<0$ is obtained by the independent increment garbling from the equality parameter $-1/\sqrt d$; hence its advantage also tends to zero. For the remaining parameters, first compare the coloring experiment with branching parameter four to a smaller-branching coloring experiment. For a regular tree keep a fixed $b$ children at each vertex, and for a Poisson tree retain each child with probability $d/4$, using the tree observation and independent randomness. This gives the smaller-tree observation without the root. Finally apply the increment garbling from $-1/2$ to the desired negative parameter. Thus all admissible negative channels with branching parameter below four are non-reconstructible as well. These are statements about the unconditional Poisson experiments, so the conclusion uses exactly the averaging convention of the problem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### Completion and the stochastic block model

Table <a href="#tab:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:coverage">1</a> collects the arguments for the equality cases. In the table, $a=|\lambda|$ denotes the negative channel magnitude.

<div id="tab:coverage">

| Equality case     | Parameter range               | Argument                                                                                                           |
|:------------------|:------------------------------|:-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Positive channel  | All admissible $d>1$          | Analytic exclusion, Section <a href="#sec:positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:positive">3</a>  |
| Negative, regular | $b\ge5$, so $a=b^{-1/2}<.448$ | Weighted identities, Section <a href="#sec:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weighted">5</a> |
| Negative, regular | $b=4$, $a=1/2$                | Finite domination, Section <a href="#grid:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:section">6</a>   |
| Negative, Poisson | $a=d^{-1/2}\le.477$           | Weighted identities, Section <a href="#sec:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weighted">5</a> |
| Negative, Poisson | $4\le d\le881/200$            | Finite domination, Section <a href="#grid:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:section">6</a>   |

Coverage of $d\lambda^2=1$, with $a=|\lambda|$ on the negative side. The two Poisson ranges overlap since $881/200>(1000/477)^2$. Below branching parameter four, negative channels follow by degradation from the coloring case.

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-grid-negative-completion-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-grid-negative-completion</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:threshold">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#fnd:supercritical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fnd:supercritical">9</a> proves reconstruction whenever $d\lambda^2>1$. Conversely, Proposition <a href="#pos:nonreconstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pos:nonreconstruction">13</a> proves non-reconstruction for $0\le\lambda\le d^{-1/2}$, and Proposition <a href="#grid:negative-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:negative-completion">22</a> proves it for every admissible negative parameter with $d\lambda^2\le1$. These conclusions include all equality cases and exhaust the channel domain. The Poisson statements use the original tree law, without conditioning on survival. ◻

</details>

We return to the stochastic block model of Section <a href="#sec:sbm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sbm">1.4</a>, where $a,b>0$ are the within- and between-community connectivity parameters and $d=(a+2b)/3>1$. The remaining step is to apply the external tree-to-graph and algorithmic results to the tree theorem.

<details id="proof-stmt-grid-negative-completion-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-grid-negative-completion</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:sbm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:sbm">2</a>.* Set $$\lambda=\frac{a-b}{a+2b}\in(-1/2,1),\qquad
 d\lambda^2=\frac{(a-b)^2}{3(a+2b)}.$$ The corresponding Poisson broadcast process has exactly the transition matrix <a href="#eq:channel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:channel">[eq:channel]</a>. Suppose first that $d\lambda^2\le1$. Theorem <a href="#thm:threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:threshold">1</a> gives $a_\ell\to0$ for this process.

To match the fixed-tree formulation of non-reconstruction, let $T$ be the entire Poisson tree and put $$A_\ell(T)=\mathbb E\!\left[
 \left\|\mathbb P(\sigma_\rho=\cdot\mid T,\sigma_{\partial T_\ell})
       -\operatorname{Unif}\{1,2,3\}\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \mathrel{\Big|}T\right].$$ For almost every fixed $T$, the boundary experiment is degraded as $\ell$ increases, so $A_\ell(T)$ decreases to a nonnegative limit $A_\infty(T)$. The offspring beyond depth $\ell$ are independent of the root and depth-$\ell$ spins conditional on $T_{\le\ell}$, and hence $\mathbb EA_\ell(T)=a_\ell$. Bounded convergence therefore gives $\mathbb EA_\infty(T)=0$, so $A_\infty(T)=0$ for almost every $T$. If $\mu_{\ell,i}^T$ is the boundary law conditional on root state $i$ and $\overline\mu_\ell^T=\frac13\sum_i\mu_{\ell,i}^T$, Bayes’ formula gives $$A_\ell(T)=\frac13\sum_{i=1}^3
   d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu_{\ell,i}^T,\overline\mu_\ell^T),$$ where $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$ denotes total-variation distance. The triangle inequality now shows that the pairwise total-variation distances between the $\mu_{\ell,i}^T$ tend to zero for almost every $T$. This is the non-reconstruction hypothesis of the tree-to-graph transfer of Mossel, Sly, and Sohn (Mossel, Sly, and Sohn 2025, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[MosselSlySohn2025](#ref-MosselSlySohn2025)]. Their conclusion, with the overlap of their Definition 1.4, gives $$\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb E\operatorname{ov}(\sigma,\widehat\sigma_n)\le\frac13$$ for deterministic estimators. Any violation by randomized estimators would, for each $n$, admit a deterministic choice of the independent seed with expected overlap at least the randomized expectation minus $1/n$, contradicting the same bound. For every pair of labelings, the average agreement over all six permutations is $1/3$, so its maximum is at least $1/3$. This proves (i).

If $d\lambda^2>1$, Corollary 1 of Abbe and Sandon (Abbe and Sandon 2018) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[AbbeSandon2018](#ref-AbbeSandon2018)], with three communities, gives an $O(n\log n)$ algorithm for detection. Their Lemma 1 converts their two-set detection criterion, for uniform community probabilities, into overlap exceeding $1/3$ by a fixed positive amount with probability tending to one. This proves (ii). ◻

</details>

The corollary covers both $a>b$ and $a<b$. Its positive-parameter hypothesis is part of the statement: the boundary cases $a=0$ or $b=0$ are not included. In particular, the negative equality has $d>4$ here; the coloring equality $a=0$, $b=6$, $d=4$ is excluded.

<a id="rad:section"></a>
### The radial inequality

This appendix proves the pointwise inequality used for positive channel parameters. Its algebraic certificates are given explicitly, so the proof requires no numerical or computer-assisted verification.

<a id="stmt-rad-lemma"></a>
<div id="rad:lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="rad:inequality"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Radial inequality). *Let $m=(m_1,m_2,m_3)$ have positive coordinates and $\langle m\rangle=1$, where $\langle w\rangle=\frac13\sum_{i=1}^3w_i$. Put $$v=m-1,\qquad x=\frac12\langle v^2\rangle,\qquad
 g=\langle \log m\rangle,\qquad u_i=\log m_i-g,$$ $$J=\frac12\langle v\log m\rangle,\qquad B=\frac12\langle m u^2\rangle,
 \qquad F(m)=3g+4J+\frac45B.$$ Since $I=g+2J$, this is the functional $F$ in Equation <a href="#pos:functional-F" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pos:functional-F">[pos:functional-F]</a>. Then, for every $0<\lambda<1$, $$\label{rad:inequality}
 F(m)-\lambda^{-2}F(1+\lambda v)
 \geq \frac{1-\lambda^2}{2}\,x^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rad-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-rad-lemma</summary>

<a id="rad:differential-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Fix the message in the statement and write $$m(\tau)=1+\tau v^{(0)},\qquad x(\tau)=\tau^2x_0,
 \qquad \mathcal F(\tau)=F(m(\tau)),\qquad 0\leq\tau\leq1,$$ where $v^{(0)}=m-1$ and $x_0=\frac12\langle (v^{(0)})^2\rangle$. Every coordinate of $m(\tau)$ is positive on this interval. The functions are analytic near $\tau=0$. Since $\langle v^{(0)}\rangle=0$, we have $g(\tau)=O(\tau^2)$, $J(\tau)=O(\tau^2)$ and $B(\tau)=O(\tau^2)$, so $\mathcal F(\tau)=O(\tau^2)$. Let $\partial=\tau\frac{d}{d\tau}$. We claim that it suffices to prove $$\label{rad:differential-bound}
 \partial(\partial-1)(\partial-2)\mathcal F(\tau)
 \geq12x(\tau)^2\qquad(0<\tau\leq1).$$ To see this, suppose the bound holds along the ray. Define $$Y=(\partial-2)\mathcal F-x(\tau)^2,\qquad Z=(\partial-1)Y.$$ The operator $\partial-2$ annihilates the quadratic Taylor term, giving $Y=O(\tau^3)$ and $Z=O(\tau^3)$ as $\tau\downarrow0$. Since $\partial(\partial-1)x(\tau)^2=12x(\tau)^2$, Equation <a href="#rad:differential-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:differential-bound">[rad:differential-bound]</a> gives $\partial Z\geq0$. For $\tau>0$ this means $Z'(\tau)\geq0$, and $Z(0)=0$ therefore gives $Z\geq0$. Next, $$\left(\frac{Y(\tau)}{\tau}\right)'
   =\frac{Z(\tau)}{\tau^2}\geq0.$$ The limit of $Y(\tau)/\tau$ at zero is zero, so $Y\geq0$. Finally, $$\frac{d}{d\tau}
 \left[\frac{\mathcal F(\tau)-\tfrac12\tau^4x_0^2}{\tau^2}\right]
 =\frac{Y(\tau)}{\tau^3}\geq0.$$ Evaluating the resulting monotonicity between $\tau=\lambda$ and $\tau=1$ gives $$F(m)-\frac12x_0^2
 \geq\lambda^{-2}F(1+\lambda v^{(0)})
          -\frac12\lambda^2x_0^2,$$ which is Equation <a href="#rad:inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:inequality">[rad:inequality]</a>.

<a id="rad:differentiation"></a>
<a id="rad:G"></a>
It remains to establish Equation <a href="#rad:differential-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:differential-bound">[rad:differential-bound]</a> for every positive message. All quantities in the following calculation refer to the current message on the ray. Set $$y=\frac12\langle v^3\rangle.$$ Thus $\partial v=v$, $\partial x=2x$, and $\partial y=3y$. Introduce $$U_j=\langle m^{-j}\rangle,\qquad S_j=\langle u m^{-j}\rangle,\qquad
 U=U_1,\quad S=S_1,\quad M=2J,\quad V=2B,\quad W=\langle u^2\rangle.$$ In particular $M=\langle m u\rangle$, since $\langle u\rangle=0$, and $F=3g+2M+\frac25V$. The identities $$\partial\log m_i=1-m_i^{-1},\qquad
 \partial u_i=U-m_i^{-1}$$ give $$\label{rad:differentiation}
\begin{aligned}
 \partial g&=1-U,&
 \partial M&=M+U-1,\\
 \partial V&=V-W+2UM,&
 \partial W&=-2S,\\
 \partial S&=U^2-U_2-S+S_2,&
 \partial U_j&=j(U_{j+1}-U_j).
\end{aligned}$$ For example, differentiating $V=\langle m u^2\rangle$ gives $\langle v u^2\rangle+2\langle m u(U-m^{-1})\rangle=V-W+2UM$; differentiating $S$ gives the next-to-last identity in <a href="#rad:differentiation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:differentiation">[rad:differentiation]</a>. Applying these identities to $F$ yields $$\label{rad:G}
\begin{aligned}
 G&:=\partial(\partial-1)(\partial-2)F\\
  &=6-12U+8U_2-2U_3\\
  &\quad+\frac45\bigl[S_2-3S+(2U_3-3U_2)M
                    +3(U-1)(U_2-U)\bigr].
\end{aligned}$$ Our remaining goal is $G\geq12x^2$.

<a id="rad:cubic"></a>
The elementary symmetric identities for the three zero-sum coordinates $v_i$ are $$\label{rad:cubic}
 v_i^3=3xv_i+2y,\qquad
 D_t:=\prod_{i=1}^3(1+t v_i)=1-3t^2x+2t^3y.$$ Write $D=D_1$. For $0\leq t\leq1$ all the factors in $D_t$ are positive and have arithmetic mean one, so <a id="rad:denominator-range"></a>$$\label{rad:denominator-range}
 0<D_t\leq1.$$ Define the coordinate polynomials $$h_i=1-v_i+v_i^2-3x,\qquad
 k_i(t)=v_i-t(v_i^2-2x).$$ The product of the two factors $1+v_j$ with $j\ne i$ is $h_i$, hence $m_i^{-1}=h_i/D$. Similarly, centering the logarithmic integral gives <a id="rad:log-integral"></a>$$\label{rad:log-integral}
 \frac{v_i}{1+t v_i}-\langle \frac{v}{1+t v}\rangle
   =\frac{k_i(t)}{D_t},\qquad
 u_i=\int_0^1\frac{k_i(t)}{D_t}\,dt.$$ The first identity follows by multiplying by $D_t$ and using <a href="#rad:cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:cubic">[rad:cubic]</a>; the second follows by integration. All denominators here are bounded away from zero for the fixed positive message under consideration.

<a id="rad:RN-representation"></a>
<a id="rad:RN-definition"></a>
<a id="rad:L-definition"></a>
<a id="rad:polynomial-goal"></a>
Put $H_j=\langle h^j\rangle$. Substitution of these formulas into <a href="#rad:G" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:G">[rad:G]</a> separates its polynomial and logarithmic terms: $$\label{rad:RN-representation}
 D^3(G-12x^2)=R+\int_0^1\frac{N(t)}{D_t}\,dt,$$ where $$\label{rad:RN-definition}
\begin{aligned}
 R={}&(6-12x^2)D^3-12H_1D^2+8H_2D-2H_3
       +\frac{12}{5}(H_1-D)(H_2-H_1D),\\
 N(t)={}&\frac45\langle k(t)
             \bigl[h^2D-3hD^2+(2H_3-3H_2D)v\bigr]\rangle.
\end{aligned}$$ In particular $N(t)$ is affine in $t$. Let $$\label{rad:L-definition}
 L=R+\int_0^1N(t)(2-D_t)\,dt.$$ Since $$\frac1{D_t}-(2-D_t)=\frac{(1-D_t)^2}{D_t}\geq0,$$ it suffices for <a href="#rad:differential-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:differential-bound">[rad:differential-bound]</a> to prove $$\label{rad:polynomial-goal}
 N(0)\geq0,\qquad N(1)\geq0,\qquad L\geq0.$$ Indeed, the first two inequalities imply $N(t)\geq0$ on $[0,1]$, and then <a href="#rad:RN-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:RN-representation">[rad:RN-representation]</a> is at least $L$.

<a id="rad:H-expansions"></a>
<a id="rad:N-expansion"></a>
<a id="rad:N1"></a>
<a id="rad:L-expansion"></a>
We give the polynomial computations and their sign certificates in full. For their verification one only needs the recurrence $$P_0=1,\quad P_1=0,\quad P_2=2x,\qquad
 P_j=3xP_{j-2}+2yP_{j-3}\quad(j\geq3),
 \qquad P_j=\langle v^j\rangle.$$ The remaining moments that occur in <a href="#rad:RN-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:RN-definition">[rad:RN-definition]</a> are $$P_3=2y,\quad P_4=6x^2,\quad P_5=10xy,\quad
 P_6=18x^3+4y^2.$$ Expanding $h^j$ and using these moments gives $$\label{rad:H-expansions}
\begin{aligned}
 H_1&=1-x,\\
 H_2&=3x^2-4y+1,\\
 H_3&=-9x^3+9x^2+6xy+3x+4y^2-14y+1.
\end{aligned}$$ The two coefficients of the affine polynomial $N(t)$ are explicitly $$\label{rad:N-expansion}
\begin{aligned}
 \frac5{24}N(t)={}&A(x,y)+t C(x,y),\\
 A(x,y)={}&3x^4+12x^3+x^2
   +(-6x^3-28x^2-2x)y+(26x-2)y^2-4y^3,\\
 C(x,y)={}&6x^4-2x^3
   +(-13x^3-10x^2-x)y+(10x^2+22x+4)y^2-16y^3.
\end{aligned}$$ In particular their sum factors as $$\label{rad:N1}
 \frac5{24}N(1)=(x-y)(9x-10y+1)(x^2+x-2y).$$ Integrating <a href="#rad:N-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:N-expansion">[rad:N-expansion]</a> against $2-D_t$ in <a href="#rad:L-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:L-definition">[rad:L-definition]</a>, and using <a href="#rad:H-expansions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:H-expansions">[rad:H-expansions]</a>, gives $$\label{rad:L-expansion}
\begin{aligned}
 \frac{25}{6}L={}&1500x^5-1020x^4+240x^3+168y^4\\
 &+(-480x^2-756x-52)y^3\\
 &+(2114x^3+710x^2+48x-80)y^2\\
 &+(-3093x^4+736x^3-35x^2)y.
\end{aligned}$$ These are finite polynomial identities: the displayed moment recurrence provides a direct way to check every coefficient.

We next describe the domain on which their signs are required. Positivity and $\sum_i m_i=3$ imply $\sum_i m_i^2<9$, and therefore $0\leq x<1$. The discriminant identity for <a href="#rad:cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:cubic">[rad:cubic]</a> is $$\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq3}(v_i-v_j)^2=108(x^3-y^2),$$ so $|y|\leq x^{3/2}$. Consequently each factor in <a href="#rad:N1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:N1">[rad:N1]</a> is nonnegative, as is seen from $$\begin{aligned}
 x-y&\geq x(1-\sqrt{x}),\\
 9x-10y+1&\geq(1-\sqrt{x})(10x+\sqrt{x}+1),\\
 x^2+x-2y&\geq x(1-\sqrt{x})^2.
\end{aligned}$$ This proves $N(1)\geq0$.

<a id="rad:domain"></a>
<a id="rad:z-substitution"></a>
If $x=0$, then $v=0$ and the lemma is immediate, so henceforth take $x>0$. Set $r=y/x$. Then $r^2\leq x$ and $r\leq\sqrt{x}<1$, while $D>0$ gives $$\label{rad:domain}
 r^2\leq x<\frac1{3-2r}.$$ The denominator is positive. Combining these inequalities yields $$0<1-r^2(3-2r)=(1-r)^2(2r+1),$$ and hence $-\frac12<r<1$. Every point of <a href="#rad:domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:domain">[rad:domain]</a> is represented by $$\label{rad:z-substitution}
 x=\frac{r^2+z/(3-2r)}{1+z},\qquad y=rx,\qquad
 z=\frac{x-r^2}{1/(3-2r)-x}\geq0.$$ Thus the parametrization includes $x=r^2$ without a limiting argument; the excluded upper endpoint corresponds to a zero coordinate of $m$.

<a id="rad:N0-certificate"></a>
For clarity write $\widetilde N_0(r,z)$ and $\widetilde L(r,z)$ for $N(0)$ and $L$ after the substitution <a href="#rad:z-substitution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:z-substitution">[rad:z-substitution]</a>, and keep $x$ on the left sides below as the expression in that substitution. The exact coefficient identity for $N(0)$ is $$\label{rad:N0-certificate}
 (1+z)^2(3-2r)^2\frac{5\widetilde N_0}{24x^2}
   =a_0(r)+a_1(r)z+a_2(r)z^2,$$ where $$\begin{aligned}
 a_0(r)&=(1-r)^3(3-2r)^2(10r^2+r+1),\\
 a_1(r)&=2(1-r)^3(3-2r)(9+5r+20r^2-4r^3),\\
 a_2(r)&=48(1-r)^3.
\end{aligned}$$ All three coefficients are nonnegative for $-\frac12\leq r\leq1$. For the first, the quadratic $10r^2+r+1$ has positive leading coefficient and discriminant $1-40=-39<0$. For the second, $$9+5r+20r^2-4r^3=9+5r+r^2(20-4r)\geq\frac{13}{2}.$$ The remaining factors in these expressions are visibly nonnegative. Since the multiplier on the left of <a href="#rad:N0-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:N0-certificate">[rad:N0-certificate]</a> is positive on our domain, $N(0)\geq0$ follows.

<a id="rad:L-certificate"></a>
The corresponding identity for $L$ is $$\label{rad:L-certificate}
 (1+z)^3(3-2r)^3\frac{25\widetilde L}{6x^2}
 =b_0(r)+b_1(r)z+b_2(r)z^2+b_3(r)z^3,$$ where $$\begin{aligned}
 b_0(r)&=r^2(1-r)^3(3-2r)^3 f_0(r),\\
 b_1(r)&=(1-r)^3(3-2r)^2 f_1(r),\\
 b_2(r)&=2(1-r)^3(3-2r) f_2(r),\\
 b_3(r)&=24(1-r)^3(25-4r^2-10r),
\end{aligned}$$ and every coefficient of the three remaining polynomials is as follows: <a id="rad:f-polynomials"></a>$$\label{rad:f-polynomials}
\begin{aligned}
 f_0(r)&=480r^4-842r^3-117r^2+445r+160,\\
 f_1(r)&=336r^6-1008r^5+1096r^4-896r^3
                  +63r^2+685r+240,\\
 f_2(r)&=440r^4-1104r^3+368r^2+413r+210.
\end{aligned}$$ Identities <a href="#rad:N0-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:N0-certificate">[rad:N0-certificate]</a> and <a href="#rad:L-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:L-certificate">[rad:L-certificate]</a> follow by substituting <a href="#rad:z-substitution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:z-substitution">[rad:z-substitution]</a> into <a href="#rad:N-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:N-expansion">[rad:N-expansion]</a> and <a href="#rad:L-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:L-expansion">[rad:L-expansion]</a>, respectively, and clearing the displayed denominators. The factor $25-4r^2-10r$ is at least $11$ on $[-\frac12,1]$: its derivative is $-8r-10<0$ throughout that interval. It remains to establish the signs of $f_0,f_1,f_2$.

Use the bijective change of variable $$r=\frac{p-1/2}{1+p},\qquad
 p=\frac{r+1/2}{1-r}\geq0
 \quad\left(-\frac12\leq r<1\right).$$ The resulting polynomial identities, with all coefficients displayed, are <a id="rad:p-certificates"></a>$$\label{rad:p-certificates}
\begin{aligned}
 4(1+p)^4 f_0\!\left(\frac{p-1/2}{1+p}\right)
    & =504p^4+4386p^3+12267p^2-1161p+174,\\
 4(1+p)^6 f_1\!\left(\frac{p-1/2}{1+p}\right)
    & =2064p^6+15486p^5+36669p^4+31368p^3\\
    &\qquad+20184p^2-2724p+522,\\
 4(1+p)^4 f_2\!\left(\frac{p-1/2}{1+p}\right)
    & =1308p^4+7650p^3+12366p^2-1842p+1044.
\end{aligned}$$ Each right side is a polynomial with nonnegative coefficients in degrees at least three, plus a strictly positive quadratic. The exact discriminants of those quadratic tails are $$\begin{aligned}
 (-1161)^2-4\cdot12267\cdot174&=-7\,189\,911<0,\\
 (-2724)^2-4\cdot20184\cdot522&=-34\,724\,016<0,\\
 (-1842)^2-4\cdot12366\cdot1044&=-48\,247\,452<0.
\end{aligned}$$ Their leading coefficients are positive, so all three tails are strictly positive on the real line. Thus $f_i(r)>0$ for $-\frac12\leq r<1$; their values at $r=1$ are, respectively, $126,516,327$, also positive. Every $b_j(r)$ is consequently nonnegative on $[-\frac12,1]$. Equation <a href="#rad:L-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:L-certificate">[rad:L-certificate]</a> proves $L\geq0$, finishing <a href="#rad:polynomial-goal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:polynomial-goal">[rad:polynomial-goal]</a> and hence <a href="#rad:differential-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rad:differential-bound">[rad:differential-bound]</a>. The initial reduction now proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="int:certificate"></a>
### Correctness of the interval certificate

<span id="int:appendix" label="int:appendix"></span>

This appendix explains the integer interval certificate for the strict positivity of the polynomial <a href="#wei:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:polynomial">[wei:polynomial]</a>. The coefficients are those in <a href="#wei:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:coefficients">[wei:coefficients]</a>, with the matching parameter choices in <a href="#wei:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:parameters">[wei:parameters]</a>. The certified domain consists of every probability vector on three states, every $\xi\in[3/100,1]$, and each of the three closed $a$ intervals $$[0,190/1000],\qquad [190/1000,448/1000],\qquad
 [448/1000,477/1000].$$ The third interval uses the Poisson values $H=1/12$, $U=1/2$; the first two use $H=1/16$, $U=15/32$. The source is supplied unchanged as `verification/interval.cpp`; see also Section <a href="#cert:interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:interval">11.2</a>. Assertions and signed 128-bit integer arithmetic are part of the certificate. We first explain what its arithmetic computes and then why normal completion proves the claimed inequality over the entire domain.

<a id="int:parametrization"></a>
#### Coordinates, formulas, and boundary extensions

<a id="int:coordinates"></a>
The quantities being checked are invariant under permutation of the three coordinates. After sorting a probability vector in decreasing order, it has the representation $$p=\frac{(1,q^{18},r^{18})}{Z},\qquad
 Z=1+q^{18}+r^{18},\qquad 0\le r\le q\le1.
 \label{int:coordinates}$$ Indeed, the largest probability is positive; take the eighteenth roots of the other two probabilities divided by it. The formulas below are valid on the larger square $0\le q,r\le1$, which will allow rectangular intervals that cross the diagonal $r=q$.

<a id="int:weighted-functions"></a>
Set $m_i=3p_i$ and $v_i=m_i-1$. Then $$v=\left(\frac3Z-1,\frac{3q^{18}}Z-1,
                  \frac{3r^{18}}Z-1\right),\qquad
 x=\frac16\sum_{i=1}^3v_i^2,\qquad y=\frac12v_1v_2v_3.$$ In particular, $-1\le v_i\le2$ everywhere on the square. For positive $q,r$, write $L_q=\log q$ and $L_r=\log r$. The centered logarithms $u_i=\log m_i-\frac13\sum_j\log m_j$ are $$(-6(L_q+L_r),\ 12L_q-6L_r,\ 12L_r-6L_q).$$ Consequently the functions used in the weighted argument are exactly $$\begin{aligned}
 G&=\frac{3q^6r^6}{Z},& T&=1-G,\notag\\
 W&=\frac{216}{Z}q^6r^6(L_q^2+L_r^2-L_qL_r),\notag\\
 Y&=\frac{648}{Z}q^6r^6
    (2L_q^3+2L_r^3-3L_q^2L_r-3L_qL_r^2).
 \label{int:weighted-functions}
\end{aligned}$$ These expressions explain the constants $216$ and $648$ in `coeff`. The entries returned by `qt` are enclosures of $q^{18}$ and $q^6(\log q)^j$, $0\le j\le3$, together with their derivatives; the analogous entries are used for $r$.

<a id="int:edge-functions"></a>
For the edge functions put $$e_i=1-av_i,\qquad G_e=(e_1e_2e_3)^{1/3},\qquad
 \mathcal L(t)=\begin{cases}-\log(1-t)/t,&t\ne0,\\1,&t=0.
 \end{cases}$$ Define $$b_i=-v_i\mathcal L(av_i),\qquad
 w_i=b_i-\frac13\sum_jb_j.$$ For $a>0$, $b_i=\log(e_i)/a$, so the centered edge logarithms are $aw_i$. Since $\sum_i v_i=0$, $$G_e^3=1-3a^2x-2a^3y.$$ Factoring $1-G_e^3$ gives the formulas used in the code: $$\widehat T=\frac{3x+2ay}{1+G_e+G_e^2},\qquad
 \widehat W=\frac{G_e}{3}\sum_iw_i^2,\qquad
 \widehat Y=aG_ew_1w_2w_3.
 \label{int:edge-functions}$$ Here $\widehat T=T_e/a^2$, $\widehat W=W_e/a^2$, and $\widehat Y=Y_e/a^2$ when $a>0$. The last identity uses $\frac13\sum_iw_i^3=w_1w_2w_3$ for three centered coordinates. Thus the code variables `Te`, `We`, and `Ye` are the hatted quantities. At $a=0$, the formulas give continuously $$G_e=1,\qquad \widehat T=x,\qquad
 \widehat W=2x,\qquad \widehat Y=0.$$ There is no division by $a$ in the implemented expressions. Substituting <a href="#int:weighted-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="int:weighted-functions">[int:weighted-functions]</a> and <a href="#int:edge-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="int:edge-functions">[int:edge-functions]</a> into the four entries returned by `coeff` gives, term by term, $(D_0,D_1,D_2,D_3)$ in <a href="#wei:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wei:coefficients">[wei:coefficients]</a>. In the parameter structure, `del`, `ch`, and `cu` mean $\delta,H,U$, and the code forms $s_0=c_0+1/3$.

<a id="int:log-product-derivative"></a>
All boundary probability vectors are included. Each function $q^6(\log q)^j$, $0\le j\le3$, is assigned value zero at $q=0$. Its derivative for $q>0$ is $$6q^5(\log q)^j+jq^5(\log q)^{j-1},
 \label{int:log-product-derivative}$$ where the second term is omitted when $j=0$. Both the function and its derivative tend to zero at zero. Thus the expressions have continuous first derivatives up to the boundary; when $qr=0$ they give $T=1$ and $W=Y=0$. Since $Z\ge1$ and $e_i>0$ throughout the domain, all four $D_i$ have the same regularity, including at $a=0$.

<a id="int:arithmetic"></a>
#### Integer intervals and machine safety

Let $S=2^{40}$. An object `I` with integer endpoints $(l,h)$ represents $[l/S,h/S]$. The functions `unit` and `integer` represent a grid point and a small integer, respectively. With positive denominator, `rat`$(b,c)$ represents $$\left[\frac{\lfloor Sb/c\rfloor}{S},
       \frac{\lceil Sb/c\rceil}{S}\right].$$ The helpers `mf`$(b,c)$ and `floor_div`$(b,c)$ compute the mathematical floors of $bc/S$ and $b/c$, also for negative numerators: the negative branch uses the negative of the corresponding positive ceiling. Upper endpoints are obtained by negating a floor of a negated expression. Addition and subtraction are exact on the grid. Multiplication chooses the appropriate endpoint extrema in each sign case; when both intervals cross zero it takes the minimum of the two potentially negative products and the maximum of the two potentially positive products. The square operation assigns lower endpoint zero when its input crosses zero.

For a positive interval $[l/S,h/S]$, the reciprocal is enclosed by $$\left[\frac{\lfloor S^2/h\rfloor}{S},
       \frac{\lceil S^2/l\rceil}{S}\right].$$ Negative reciprocals use odd symmetry. An assertion excludes intervals containing zero. Consequently all elementary interval operations contain their exact real counterparts. Intersecting a value interval with a separately established range is also sound. The two uses of this operation, `clip`, are the range $[-1,2]$ for $v_i$ and the logarithmic-product bounds proved below.

These statements concern exact integer operations, so absence of overflow must be established before using their outputs. Every potentially growing endpoint calculation is passed through `safe`, which asserts $$-2^{62}<b<2^{62}$$ before converting the signed 128-bit integer $b$ to a signed 64-bit endpoint. Inductively, two input endpoints have product of magnitude less than $2^{124}$. The product is formed after promoting to signed 128 bits, and the rounding offset $S-1$ still leaves its magnitude below $2^{127}$. Endpoint addition is also promoted before checking. Reciprocal numerators are $S^2=2^{80}$; adding the denominator minus one for upward rounding is safe. Rational-construction numerators and denominators in the prescribed runs are small fixed integers. No unchecked negation or absolute value can encounter the minimum signed 64-bit integer, because endpoints have magnitude below $2^{62}$.

The remaining raw integer expressions have separate small bounds. All box coordinates lie in $[0,S]$ in grid units, so midpoint sums are at most $2S$. Cube-root search candidates are below $2S$, their endpoint sums are at most $4S$, and the two cube-comparison operands are below $8S^3=2^{123}$. The fixed multiples of $S$, loop indices, and initial-grid numerators fit signed 64 bits. The unchecked visit counter is treated separately by the concrete sanitized executions recorded below; the depth bound alone does not keep its total number of increments within the signed 64-bit range. Logarithm scaling uses at most forty doublings on the actual arguments. Thus a range assertion is never being asked to detect an overflow that has already occurred: oversized derivative or value enclosures stop at `safe` before narrowing. All floating-point casts in this source occur in failure diagnostics, not in acceptance tests.

<a id="int:logs"></a>
#### Logarithms, divided logarithms, and cube roots

The function `atanh2` evaluates the fifteen terms $$2\sum_{n=0}^{14}\frac{w^{2n+1}}{2n+1}$$ by Horner’s rule with outward interval operations. For $|w|\le u<1$ the absolute remainder is at most $$\frac{2u^{31}}{31(1-u^2)}.$$ The code asserts $|w|\le1/3+4/S<3/8$. With $u=3/8$, the remainder bound, in grid units, is the exact rational number <a id="int:atanh-tail"></a>$$S\frac{2(3/8)^{31}}{31(1-(3/8)^2)}
 =\frac{617673396283947}{119978708822917120}
 <\frac1{100}<2.
 \label{int:atanh-tail}$$ The padding $[-2/S,2/S]$ therefore covers the omitted tail; all polynomial-evaluation errors are already covered by interval rounding.

To enclose $\log(x/S)$ for a positive integer endpoint $x$, `ln` scales an interval containing $x/S$ by powers of two. If $m$ is the resulting interval and $k$ the accumulated exponent, then $x/S\in2^km$. Multiplication by two is exact on this grid; halving, when used, rounds outwards. The identities $$\log t=2\operatorname{atanh}\frac{t-1}{t+1},\qquad
 \log2=2\operatorname{atanh}\frac13$$ give the enclosure after adding $k\log2$. The domain assertion in `atanh2` checks the needed range even after rounding. All positive arguments actually passed to `ln` are either coordinate endpoints in $[1/S,1]$, or endpoints of $1-av_i$ bounded away from zero, so the scaling loops terminate within the stated bounds.

The divided logarithm has the integral and power-series representations <a id="int:L-representation"></a>$$\mathcal L(t)=\int_0^1\frac{ds}{1-st}
             =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^n}{n+1}
 \quad (|t|<1).
 \label{int:L-representation}$$ For $|t|\le1/4$, `L_endpoint` evaluates $\sum_{n=0}^{22}t^n/(n+1)$ and its derivative $\sum_{n=1}^{22}nt^{n-1}/(n+1)$ independently. Bounding both coefficient sequences by one bounds their absolute tails by <a id="int:L-tails"></a>$$\frac{(1/4)^{23}}{1-1/4}=\frac1{48S},\qquad
 \frac{(1/4)^{22}}{1-1/4}=\frac1{12S},
 \label{int:L-tails}$$ respectively. Each receives padding $[-1/S,1/S]$. Outside this small interval the exact identities $$\mathcal L(t)=-\frac{\log(1-t)}t,\qquad
 \mathcal L'(t)=\frac{(1-t)^{-1}-\mathcal L(t)}t$$ use only nonzero denominators. On the entire real domain $t<1$, differentiation of the integral representation gives $$\mathcal L'(t)=\int_0^1\frac{s\,ds}{(1-st)^2}>0,\qquad
 \mathcal L''(t)=2\int_0^1\frac{s^2\,ds}{(1-st)^3}>0.$$ Thus both $\mathcal L$ and $\mathcal L'$ increase, including at negative arguments. The lower enclosure at the left endpoint and upper enclosure at the right endpoint suffice to enclose each function on an interval. This is precisely the rule used by `L`.

For a nonnegative integer $x<8S$, `cb_floor` uses binary search and the exact comparison $$m^3\le xS^2$$ to return $\lfloor S(x/S)^{1/3}\rfloor$. Its initial search bracket $[0,2S]$ is valid by $x<8S$. Applying this function to the two input endpoints, then increasing the upper result by one grid unit, encloses the cube root. The derivative enclosure is obtained from $1/(3t^{2/3})$ using the already enclosed positive root.

The outward rounding of an $a$ endpoint enlarges the prescribed bands by less than $1/S$, and never introduces negative $a$. Hence throughout the actual boxes $a\le477/1000+1/S$. Even the interval enclosures of $1-av_i$, after multiplication rounding and clipping $v_i$ to $[-1,2]$, have lower endpoint greater than $45/1000$ and upper endpoint less than $1478/1000$. The successive product of the three factors has lower endpoint at least $(45/1000)^3-3/S>0$ and upper endpoint at most $(1478/1000)^3+3/S<8$. These bounds justify the logarithm, divided-logarithm, reciprocal, and cube-root domains on every initial box and descendant. The cube-root lower enclosure is positive as well, so its derivative reciprocal is valid.

<a id="int:derivatives"></a>
#### Logarithmic products and derivative propagation

The zero endpoints in <a href="#int:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="int:coordinates">[int:coordinates]</a> are treated without evaluating their logarithms. For $0<q\le1$, set $t=-\log q\ge0$. For integers $0\le j\le3$ and real $h\ge5$, $$q^{h/2}|\log q|^j=e^{-ht/2}t^j\le1.$$ For $j=0$ this is immediate. For $j>0$ its maximum is $(2j/(he))^j\le1$, since $2j/h\le6/5<e$. It follows that <a id="int:qt-bound"></a>$$|q^5(\log q)^j|\le q^{5/2}\le q^2,\qquad
 |q^6(\log q)^j|\le q^3.
 \label{int:qt-bound}$$ For even $j$ the products are nonnegative, and for odd $j$ they are nonpositive. These are the exact external ranges used by `qt_direct` to intersect the power-and-logarithm enclosures. When the lower endpoint is zero, the same ranges directly supply an enclosure, with no logarithm call. When the lower endpoint is positive, monotonicity of $\log$ supplies its interval, and intersecting its upper bound with $0$ is valid because $q\le1$. The derivative returned for every logarithmic product is exactly the interval version of <a href="#int:log-product-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="int:log-product-derivative">[int:log-product-derivative]</a>. The derivative of $q^{18}$ is $18q^{17}$. Cached enclosures depend only on the interval endpoints; `qt` puts their derivatives in the appropriate $q$ or $r$ gradient coordinate.

An object `J` consists of a value interval and three derivative intervals, corresponding to $(q,r,a)$. Constants have zero gradients and variables have the appropriate unit gradient. Addition, multiplication, squares, and reciprocals apply the identities $$\partial_i(fg)=f\,\partial_i g+g\,\partial_i f,\qquad
 \partial_i(f^2)=2f\,\partial_i f,\qquad
 \partial_i(f^{-1})=-f^{-2}\partial_i f.$$ Cube roots and $\mathcal L$ use the derivative enclosures just proved and the chain rule. Induction over these operations proves that the value and every first partial derivative of the actual formula are enclosed throughout the box. Tightening a value enclosure by an external range does not replace the underlying function by a clipped function: its previously enclosed derivatives remain valid, and the tighter values can be used in subsequent product and chain rules. All these statements extend to zero coordinate endpoints by the continuous derivative extensions already established.

<a id="int:bernstein"></a>
#### Bernstein coefficients and the acceptance test

<a id="int:bernstein-rows"></a>
For fixed $(q,r,a)$ write the polynomial as $P(\xi)=D_0+D_1\xi+D_2\xi^2+D_3\xi^3$. On a subinterval $[l,h]$, substitute $\xi=l+(h-l)t$, $0\le t\le1$, and express $P$ in the basis $\binom3j t^j(1-t)^{3-j}$. The multipliers of $D_1,D_2,D_3$ in the four coefficients are $$\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 j& D_1&D_2&D_3\\ \hline
 0&l&l^2&l^3\\
 1&(2l+h)/3&l(l+2h)/3&l^2h\\
 2&(l+2h)/3&h(h+2l)/3&lh^2\\
 3&h&h^2&h^3
 \end{array}
 \label{int:bernstein-rows}$$ and the multiplier of $D_0$ is always one. Expanding the four basis polynomials verifies these identities. All basis polynomials are nonnegative and their sum is one; strictly positive coefficients therefore imply $P(\xi)>0$ on the whole closed subinterval.

<a id="int:xi-grid"></a>
The function `rows` uses successive intervals with endpoints $$\frac1{1000}(30,50,75,100,150,200,300,400,500,650,800,900,1000).
 \label{int:xi-grid}$$ These twelve intervals cover $[3/100,1]$ without gaps. For each it stores the first three rows of <a href="#int:bernstein-rows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="int:bernstein-rows">[int:bernstein-rows]</a>; the fourth row equals the first row for the next interval. It finally adds $(1,1,1)$. Thus the $37$ stored rows account for all $48$ coefficients of the twelve cubic representations, including both extreme endpoints. The row multipliers are themselves evaluated by rational interval arithmetic and have zero gradients with respect to $(q,r,a)$.

Let $F$ denote one of these Bernstein coefficients on a box $B=\prod_{i=1}^3[l_i/S,h_i/S]$. The interval calculation in `dot` encloses $F$ and all its first derivatives. A positive lower endpoint immediately accepts this coefficient. Otherwise define the grid midpoint and coordinate radii by $$c_i=\frac{\lfloor(l_i+h_i)/2\rfloor}{S},\qquad
 \rho_i=\frac{h_i-\lfloor(l_i+h_i)/2\rfloor}{S}.$$ Nonnegative endpoints ensure that C++ integer division computes this floor; $\rho_i$ is the larger of the two distances to the endpoints. If $M_i$ is the maximum absolute endpoint of the derivative enclosure, in real units, then for every $z\in B$ the segment from $c$ to $z$ lies in $B$ and <a id="int:mean-value"></a>$$F(z)\ge F(c)-\sum_{i=1}^3M_i\rho_i.
 \label{int:mean-value}$$ This follows by integrating the gradient along that segment, also when a coordinate endpoint is zero. The code encloses $F(c)$ anew, rounds each product $M_i\rho_i$ upwards, and adds their integer upper endpoints with overflow checks. Its strict comparison `value.v.l > total` is therefore sufficient for $F>0$ throughout $B$. A box is accepted only when every stored row passes one of these two tests.

<a id="int:coverage"></a>
#### Subdivision, coverage, and observed completion

<a id="int:initial-boxes"></a>
For a band $[a_-,a_+]$, the initial exact boxes before outward rounding are $$\left[\frac i{24},\frac{i+1}{24}\right]
 \times\left[\frac j{24},\frac{j+1}{24}\right]
 \times\left[a_-+\frac{k}{16}(a_+-a_-),
              a_-+\frac{k+1}{16}(a_+-a_-)\right],
 \label{int:initial-boxes}$$ where $0\le i<24$, $0\le j\le i$, and $0\le k<16$. Every $(q,r)$ with $0\le r\le q\le1$ belongs to one of the displayed $q,r$ rectangles, including all grid boundaries. There are $16\sum_{i=0}^{23}(i+1)=4800$ initial boxes per run. Their endpoints are rounded outwards, so none of the required domain is lost. The extra points included by rounding are harmless, as are portions of diagonal rectangles with $r>q$: the same functions and derivative bounds are valid there. The $a$ enlargement and all analytic domains were checked above. Adjacent bands may use different parameters, since each certifies its own entire closed band.

On entry, `visit` skips a box only if its lower $r$ endpoint exceeds its upper $q$ endpoint. Such a box contains no point with $r\le q$. If any coefficient fails both acceptance tests, the code chooses a coordinate with maximal recorded derivative-radius contribution and cuts at its grid midpoint. The choice affects efficiency only. The two closed children include the midpoint and their union is the parent. If the chosen contribution is zero, the depth exceeds $80$, or the chosen grid radius is at most one, an assertion fails rather than accepting an unresolved box.

Induction back up any normally completed recursion tree now proves that every required point in its root box has all Bernstein coefficients strictly positive. The leaves either pass the sound acceptance tests or contain no required point. Together with <a href="#int:initial-boxes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="int:initial-boxes">[int:initial-boxes]</a>, <a href="#int:xi-grid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="int:xi-grid">[int:xi-grid]</a>, and the sorted parametrization, normal completion of all three runs proves the pointwise inequality on the full stated domain. This includes the uniform message, boundary probability vectors, $a=0$, all band endpoints, and all $\xi$ endpoints. In particular there is no exception at the uniform message: there $P(\xi)=\delta\xi^2+
U\delta\xi^3>0$.

<span id="int:execution" label="int:execution"></span> The unchanged source was replayed with GCC 13.3.0 on x86_64 Linux, using assertions enabled. From the package root the reproduction commands are

    g++ -std=c++17 -O3 -UNDEBUG verification/interval.cpp \
      -o /tmp/appendix_b
    /tmp/appendix_b 0
    /tmp/appendix_b 1
    /tmp/appendix_b 2

Only the documented arguments $0,1,2$ are part of this protocol; the source does not validate other argument values. Compiling with assertions disabled is not this certificate. All three runs exited normally with status zero and the following visit counts: $$\begin{array}{c|c|r}
 \text{argument}&a\text{ interval}&\text{calls to \texttt{visit}}\\ \hline
 0&[0,190/1000]&414080\\
 1&[190/1000,448/1000]&756096\\
 2&[448/1000,477/1000]&1004426
 \end{array}$$ The counts include internal subdivision nodes and boxes skipped by the diagonal test. All three executions were also repeated on the unchanged source with `-O2 -UNDEBUG -fsanitize=undefined` and `-fno-sanitize-recover=undefined`. They completed with the same counts and no sanitizer diagnostics. This supplies the separate check of the unchecked `visits++` counter for these prescribed executions. The counts identify the finite completed executions; their correctness implication is the enclosure and coverage argument above, not a sample-based inference. The source SHA-256 is

    76d1443b1194597ee77749e8030c825f2b9aae46b5c172d9eccf0745078c28e3

<a id="grid:implementation"></a>
### Exact arithmetic and reproduction of the grid certificate

The complete C++17 program in Section <a href="#cert:grid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:grid">11.3</a> implements the recursions of Section <a href="#grid:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:section">6</a>. Its parameters are fixed at $H=72$, $N=10^{12}$, and $\mathtt{SCALE}=10^{15}$. It uses signed 64-bit integers for table coefficients and mass units, and signed 128-bit integers for products and accumulators. The compiler must also provide a plain `int` type of at least 32 bits. Assertions must be enabled; in particular the build must not define `NDEBUG`. For example, from the paper directory, the following commands compile and run all 28 cases:

    g++ -std=c++17 -O3 -UNDEBUG -Wall -Wextra \
        verification/grid.cpp -o grid
    for j in $(seq -1 26); do ./grid "$j"; done

The program writes progress diagnostics to standard error and the case number followed by its stopping iteration to standard output. Only the diagnostics convert to floating point. Successful outputs are specified in <a href="#grid:outputs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:outputs">[grid:outputs]</a>, together with `-1 162`.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Correspondence of code and probability operations

The routine `init` enumerates all sorted nonnegative integer triples of sum $72$. It finds $469$ triples, identifies the pure representative $(72,0,0)$ and the uniform representative $(24,24,24)$, and stores an index for every representative. The routine `indexof` sorts a candidate triple and verifies the resulting index. A `Law` is a vector of orbit masses in units of $1/N$. The routine `complete` adds the missing units at the pure orbit, which is exactly Lemma <a href="#grid:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:completion">21</a>.

The constructor `Table(mode,A,AD)` first reduces the channel fraction. The variables `lhs`, `rhs`, `raw`, `T`, and `den` are the entries and denominator of <a href="#grid:modes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:modes">[grid:modes]</a>. For each of the six relative permutations, `n`, `rem`, and `miss` are the floor vector, remainders, and deficit in the grid spread. The corner weights are `T`, `rem[c]`, or `T-rem[c]`, as appropriate. After aggregating equal output indices, the constructor checks that their sum is `den`. These checks include every grid pair, not only pairs visited by an iteration. Zero normalizers are omitted because they have zero tilted mass.

The routine `combine` implements <a href="#grid:combine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:combine">[grid:combine]</a>. Symmetric modes use the triangular pair table and the stated off-diagonal coefficient; mode 1 uses ordered pairs. Its row-skipping condition is safe: an ordered row contributes zero when its first mass is zero, while a triangular row contributes zero when both first-index masses are zero. The table position advances by the full skipped row length. After division by $N\,\mathtt{den}$, each mass is rounded down and `complete` is applied.

The functions `down` and `up` compute floor and ceiling of a nonnegative integer ratio. The latter uses $\lceil x/y\rceil=\lfloor(x+y-1)/y\rfloor$ for $x\geq0$, $y>0$. The function `w_zero` implements <a href="#grid:taylor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:taylor">[grid:taylor]</a>. The routine `poisson` fixes its incoming child law `inc`, starts `part` at the uniform zero-count law, and uses mode 1 once for each positive count weight. Its orbit sums are exactly <a href="#grid:mixture" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:mixture">[grid:mixture]</a>; the final `complete` handles all omitted mass. No count product is needed after the first zero weight. The regular branch of `run` is <a href="#grid:regular-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:regular-recursion">[grid:regular-recursion]</a>. Finally, `mu_num` is $M$ in <a href="#grid:moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:moment">[grid:moment]</a>, and the strict stopping test is $20M<NH^2$. Each run allows $400$ iterations and asserts failure if none succeeds. All reported stopping iterations are below that bound.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Bounds on every arithmetic class

For all Poisson table calls, the reduced fraction satisfies $B\leq10^6$ and $A\leq B/2$. Thus $$0\leq e_i\leq(B+A)H\leq108000000,
 \quad D_{\mathrm{tab}}=6BH^2\leq31104000000,
 \quad Hr_i\leq559872000000.$$ Each total $T$ is at most three times the bound on a raw coordinate; all of these quantities fit signed 64 bits. The only mode 2 call is at the reduced fraction $A/B=1/2$, where $D_{\mathrm{tab}}=373248$ and $Hr_i\leq3359232$. Mode 0 has smaller bounds. Every nonnegative aggregated table weight and every partial sum of weights is bounded by its denominator, by <a href="#grid:table-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:table-mass">[grid:table-mass]</a>.

Input masses are nonnegative and sum to $N$, so their ordered pair mass is $N^2$. The triangular implementation has the same total after the off-diagonal terms are combined. Consequently every nonnegative product accumulator and its partial sums are at most $$D_{\mathrm{tab}}N^2
 \leq31104000000000000000000000000000000<2^{127}-1.$$ The source casts an input factor to the 128-bit type `Big` before multiplying. Dividing by $ND_{\mathrm{tab}}$ yields a mass between zero and $N$, safely convertible back to 64 bits. The uncompleted masses sum to at most $N$; likewise the Poisson mixture masses sum to at most $N$, since they are lower bounds on a probability mixture. These facts bound all 64-bit partial sums in `complete` and `poisson`.

Here are bounds for the Taylor calculation independent of machine floating point. If $t_n=S_0d_+^n/n!$, the lower and upper endpoint errors satisfy $$0\leq t_n-l_n< e^{d_+},\qquad
 0\leq u_n-t_n< e^{d_+}.$$ Indeed an error satisfies $\epsilon_n\leq(d_+/n)\epsilon_{n-1}+1$; expanding this recurrence bounds it by $\sum_{j\geq0}d_+^j/j!=e^{d_+}$, since each product of $j$ successive denominators is at least $j!$. As $d_+<5$ and $e<3$, the endpoint errors are less than $243$. The sum of all endpoint magnitudes through degree $41$ is therefore bounded by $$S_0e^{d_+}+42\cdot243<243(S_0+42)<2^{63}-1.$$ This also bounds every signed partial sum in <a href="#grid:taylor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:taylor">[grid:taylor]</a>. The endpoint multiplication by $d_{\max}\leq881$ is carried out in 128 bits before division, and the same holds for multiplication by $N$ and for the Poisson weight recurrence. These products are all less than $2^{127}-1$ under the displayed bounds.

The lower sum $L$ is positive. In fact Taylor’s theorem gives $$\frac{L}{S_0}
 \geq e^{-d_+}-\frac{d_+^{42}}{42!}
                   -\frac{41\cdot243}{S_0}
 >\frac1{243}-10^{-20}-\frac{9963}{10^{15}}>0.$$ The middle inequality uses the elementary integer comparison $5^{42}/42!<10^{-20}$. Thus the nonnegative-argument conditions of `down` hold at the final Taylor step as well as in its term recurrences. Since $w_n/N\leq p_n$, every Poisson weight is at most $N$. In particular $w_8\leq N<2^{40}$, and the recurrence gives $w_n\leq w_{n-1}/2$ for $n\geq9$, since $d_+/n<1/2$. Thus $w_{48}<1$, so the first zero occurs by $n=48$. The count loop and its denominator therefore satisfy $n\leq48$ and $200n\leq9600$, well within the plain integer type. Every product $w_nq_n(h)$ is at most $N^2$ and is evaluated in 128 bits.

For $h\in\mathcal H$, the bracket in <a href="#grid:moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:moment">[grid:moment]</a> belongs to $[0,H^2]$. Hence every partial sum in `mu_num` is at most $$NH^2=5184000000000000<2^{63}-1.$$ The factor $20$ in the stopping comparison is applied after converting to `Big`. The binary-search comparisons for <a href="#grid:magnitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="grid:magnitude">[grid:magnitude]</a> likewise use 128 bits before taking integer squares and products.

There are at most $18$ unaggregated contributions per grid pair (six permutations, at most three corners). Thus even a full ordered table has at most $18\cdot469^2=3959298$ entries before aggregation. All table positions and vector indices fit an ordinary signed 32-bit integer, and the source also checks an explicit $100000000$ entry limit. Candidate grid coordinates belong to $\{0,\ldots,H\}$, and all small grid sums and products fit these integer types. This accounts for the unguarded indexing arithmetic as well as the probabilistic accumulators.

The supplied source therefore implements a finite exact-arithmetic certificate: positivity, normalization, rounding direction, all parameter choices, and the final strict inequalities are independent of compiler floating-point behavior.

<a id="app:certificates"></a>
### Exact verification programs

The three programs below are part of the proof. They use integer or rational arithmetic for every certification decision. In the C++ programs, floating-point conversion is used only for diagnostic output. The mathematical meaning and arithmetic guarantees of the programs are proved in Sections <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">4</a>–<a href="#sec:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weighted">5</a>, Section <a href="#grid:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:section">6</a>, and Appendices <a href="#int:appendix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="int:appendix">[int:appendix]</a> and <a href="#grid:implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:implementation">10</a>, and in the pair derivation below.

<a id="cert:pair"></a>
#### Reproduction protocol

The accompanying directory `verification/` contains `pair.py`, `interval.cpp`, and `grid.cpp`, exactly the sources printed below. Run Python with SymPy installed and with assertions enabled. Compile the C++ sources with a compiler supporting signed `__int128` and a plain `int` type of at least 32 bits, also with assertions enabled. For example, from the paper directory:

```
python verification/pair.py
g++ -std=c++17 -O3 -UNDEBUG verification/interval.cpp -o interval
g++ -std=c++17 -O3 -UNDEBUG verification/grid.cpp -o grid
for j in 0 1 2; do ./interval "$j"; done
for j in -1 $(seq 0 26); do ./grid "$j"; done
```

Do not use Python’s `-O` option, and do not define `NDEBUG`. Only the displayed argument ranges are part of the protocol. The bundled runner `verification/reproduce.py` verifies the source hashes, runs this suite, checks the expected outputs, and saves the commands, software versions, exit statuses, and logs in its designated output directory. Its usage is documented in the accompanying README.

Successful execution of the pair program produces no output. The interval runs finish with visited-box counts $414080$, $756096$, and $1004426$, respectively. The grid run with argument $-1$ stops at iteration $162$. The other grid stopping iterations are recorded in Section <a href="#grid:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:section">6</a>. The certificate sources were replayed with Python 3.12.13, SymPy 1.14.0, and GCC 13.3.0 on x86_64 Linux; all 32 mathematical executions and five environment/compiler checks passed. Separately, the three interval cases were replayed with `-O2 -UNDEBUG`, `-fsanitize=undefined`, and `-fno-sanitize-recover=undefined`. All three exited successfully, with the same progress logs and no sanitizer diagnostics. This supplemental check is distinct from the ordinary runner protocol and supplies the execution check on the visit counter discussed in Appendix <a href="#int:appendix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="int:appendix">[int:appendix]</a>.

The SHA-256 digests of the three source files, in the above order, are

> `ab775c3a63fb1d2713bfe058af2dece4ceda99627c17ce18f51f817b69410bba`  
> `76d1443b1194597ee77749e8030c825f2b9aae46b5c172d9eccf0745078c28e3`  
> `03c92321efedd0db53b1e91372918dfcd68d459db0223cb160c39df3bd645283`

These digests identify the exact files; the correctness arguments are provided in the text, and the successful runs establish the finite assertions required there.

<a id="cert:interval"></a>
#### Pair coefficient certificate

We prove the $a=1/2$ case of Lemma <a href="#loc:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:pair">15</a> for strictly positive input vectors, using its notation for the input invariants and pair excesses. The main-text proof then extends these bounds to every $0\le a\le1/2$ and to the closed simplex. Parametrize sorted unnormalized vectors by $$p=(6s+3t+2,3t+2,2),\qquad
 q=(6u+3v+2,3v+2,2),\qquad s,t,u,v\ge0.$$ This covers every strictly positive sorted vector up to scale: for $h_0\ge h_1\ge h_2>0$, take $s=(h_0-h_1)/(3h_2)$ and $t=2(h_1-h_2)/(3h_2)$ to obtain $p=2h/h_2$. Put $P=\sum_i p_i$, $Q=\sum_i q_i$, and $e_i=Q-q_i$, with $E=\sum_i e_i=2Q$. Then $e_i/E=(1-q_i/Q)/2$, as required for the coloring edge. For an unnormalized vector $r$ of total $R$, put $$N_x(r)=\frac{3\sum_i r_i^2-R^2}{2},\qquad
 N_y(r)=\frac{27\prod_i r_i-R^3+3R N_x(r)}2.$$ The identities for the posterior invariants give $N_x(r)=R^2x(r/R)$ and $N_y(r)=R^3y(r/R)$. These are the two numerators returned by `xy` in the source.

For each of the two choices $\widetilde e=(e_0,e_1,e_2)$ and $\widetilde e=(e_0,e_2,e_1)$, let $$r_i^{(j)}=p_i\widetilde e_{i+j},\qquad
 T_j=\sum_i r_i^{(j)},\qquad j=0,1,2,$$ with indices modulo three. The likelihood normalizer is $3T_j/(PE)$; multiplying by the cyclic probability $1/3$ gives weight $T_j/(PE)$. Since $\sum_jT_j=PE$, these are probability weights. The two choices, each followed by the three rotations, enumerate all six relative permutations. Repeated representatives at a stabilizer remain valid uniform group averages.

<a id="loc:denominator"></a>
For each reflection class separately, clear the positive denominator $$\label{loc:denominator}
 D=PE(T_0T_1T_2)^3P^4E^4.$$ The three expressions `out[0]`, `out[1]`, and `out[2]` divided by $D$ are exactly $$\sum_{j=0}^2\frac{N_x(r^{(j)})}{PE T_j},\qquad
 \sum_{j=0}^2\frac{N_y(r^{(j)})}{PE T_j^2},\qquad
 \sum_{j=0}^2\frac{N_x(r^{(j)})^2}{PE T_j^3},$$ namely the tilted output means of $x,y,x^2$ within that class. For every call in the source, $$\frac{\texttt{mon}(i,j,k,l)}D=X^i y_0^j z^kB_0^l.$$ The powers $4-2i-3j$ and $4-2k-3l$ in these calls are nonnegative, so the cleared objects are polynomials. Hence `dx`, `de`, and `dn` are $D$ times the class-specific excesses.

<a id="loc:reflection"></a>
The correction `im`, after division by $D$, is $$\label{loc:reflection}
 I=\frac{27\,\operatorname{odd}(p)\operatorname{odd}(\widetilde e)}
              {64P^3E^3},\qquad
 \operatorname{odd}(p)=4(p_1-p_2)(p_0-p_1)(p_0-p_2).$$ Indeed this follows immediately by dividing $27\operatorname{odd}(p)\operatorname{odd}(\widetilde e)
 [PE(T_0T_1T_2)^3]PE/64$ by <a href="#loc:denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:denominator">[loc:denominator]</a>. Swapping the last two edge coordinates negates $I$. Although $D$ itself depends on the reflection class, the normalized correction in <a href="#loc:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:reflection">[loc:reflection]</a> has exactly the indicated sign change.

To see explicitly what is certified, temporarily write $d_x,d_e,d_n$ for the excesses within one reflection class. The seven polynomials checked in that class are the following expressions multiplied by $D$: $$\begin{aligned}
 &13Xz-2d_n,\\
 &11Xz-d_e,\\
 &250d_e+79Xz,\\
 &-20Xz-40W+40y_0B_0+51S-20(d_x-2I),\\
 &6Xz-6W+y_0B_0+S-(d_e+I),\\
 &2(d_e+I)-12Xz+12W-2y_0B_0+19S,\\
 &-4Xz+44W-14y_0B_0+52S_A+111S_B
       -\{5(d_n+10I)-4(d_e+I)\}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last line, divided by two, bounds $Cd_n-2d_e+23I$. All arithmetic in this certificate consists of integer polynomial addition and convolution, and exact scalar division, in $\mathbb Z[s,t,u,v]$. Each of the fourteen polynomials has total degree $28$ and all its coefficients are nonnegative. For either reflection the numbers of nonzero coefficients in the displayed order are $$13672,\ 13672,\ 13674,\ 13625,\ 13625,\ 13625,\ 13625.$$ The supplied executable certificate checks every coefficient by the assertion `min(f.coeffs())>=0`; absent monomials have coefficient zero. Its unchanged-source execution, with Python assertions enabled, terminates successfully. Thus these are exact polynomial nonnegativity statements on the entire nonnegative quadrant, rather than evaluations on a set of test points.

Divide each class’s seven inequalities by its own positive $D$ and then average the two normalized classes. All right-hand invariant terms are unchanged by reflection, and the $I$ terms cancel by <a href="#loc:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:reflection">[loc:reflection]</a>. This gives precisely <a href="#loc:coarsepair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:coarsepair">[loc:coarsepair]</a>–<a href="#loc:kpair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:kpair">[loc:kpair]</a> for $a=1/2$.

          "exact_quote": "If $d\\lambda^2>1$, Corollary~1 of Abbe and Sandon\n\\cite{AbbeSandon2018}",

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Domination in the first closed Poisson bin $[4,803/200]$; the two axes use different enlarged scales. The upper corner has $a_{+} = 1/2$ and $d_{+} = 803/200$, so it dominates every equality experiment in the bin by thinning offspring and reducing the channel magnitude. It lies above the equality curve. The certificate only brings its finite-depth upper law below $μ = 1/20$; the local fixed-point gap is applied to the target equality experiment.",
      "figure_id": "fig:dominating-bin",
      "pdf_page": 32,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:dominating-bin",
          "exact_quote": "Domination in the first closed Poisson bin\n$[4,803/200]$; the two axes use different enlarged scales.\nThe upper corner has $a_+=1/2$ and $d_+=803/200$, so it dominates\nevery equality experiment in the bin by thinning offspring and reducing\n",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=6.2cm,y=2.8cm,>=stealth,font=\\small]\n  \\pgfmathsetmacro{\\targety}{1000/sqrt(4+.015*.5)-499}\n  \\pgfmathsetmacro{\\righty}{1000/sqrt(4+.015)-499}\n  \\draw[->] (-.10,-.19) -- (1.20,-.19) node[right] {$d$};\n  \\draw[->] (-.10,-.19) -- (-.10,1.34) node[above] {$a=|\\lambda|$};\n  \\draw[densely dashed,black!40] (0,-.19) -- (0,1);\n  \\draw[densely dashed,black!40] (1,-.19) -- (1,1);\n  \\draw[densely dashed,black!40] (-.10,1) -- (1,1);\n  \\node[below] at (0,-.19) {$4$};\n  \\node[below] at (1,-.19) {$803/200$};\n  \\node[left] at (-.10,1) {$1/2$};\n  \\draw[thick,blue!50!black,domain=0:1,samples=61]\n    plot (\\x,{1000/sqrt(4+.015*\\x)-499});\n  \\fill (0,1) circle (1.4pt);\n  \\fill (1,\\righty) circle (1.4pt);\n  \\fill (1,1) circle (2pt);\n  \\node[above right] at (1,1) {$(d_+,a_+)$};\n  \\draw[->,thick] (.97,1) -- (.5,1)\n    node[midway,above] {thin offspring};\n  \\draw[->,thick] (.5,.97) -- (.5,{\\targety+.035});\n  \\node[right] at (.52,.79) {reduce magnitude};\n  \\fill (.5,\\targety) circle (2pt);\n  \\node[below left] at (.5,{\\targety-.03}) {$(d,d^{-1/2})$};\n  \\node[below right,text=blue!50!black] at (.66,.20)\n    {$a=d^{-1/2}$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D plot showing domination in the parameter space:\n- Horizontal axis labeled $d$ with tick marks at $4$ and $803/200$.\n- Vertical axis labeled $a = |\\lambda|$ with a tick mark at $1/2$.\n- A curve labeled $a = d^{-1/2}$ decreasing from the point $(4, 1/2)$ down and to the right past $d = 803/200$.\n- A marked point $(d_+, a_+)$ at the upper-right corner corresponding to $d_+ = 803/200, a_+ = 1/2$.\n- A horizontal arrow pointing left from $(d_+, a_+)$ labeled \"thin offspring\" above the arrow.\n- A vertical downward arrow from the end of the horizontal path down to the curve, labeled \"reduce magnitude\" to the right of the arrow.\n- The destination on the curve is marked with a point labeled $(d, d^{-1/2})$."
    }
  ],
